The neighbours at $\psi_D\pm60^\circ$ are not in $S$, and one of them has $30^\circ<|\psi|\le60^\circ$, so $\cos\psi\ge\frac12>h$; by (F4) it is not in $\operatorname{int}K$, so it lies in $D$, and (F3) gives $|\psi|<30^\circ$, a contradiction.
\item The two pairs on one side, $\{\psi_D+60^\circ,\psi_D+120^\circ\}$ and $\{\psi_D-60^\circ,\psi_D-120^\circ\}$, are excluded by (ii).
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}
Hence $\deg y=6$ implies $m_y\le2$, and $\deg y\le5$ implies $\deg y+m_y\le8$. For $\eps_0=1/50$ the exceptional set has at most $6.89\cdot10^7+5106+465^2<7.1\cdot10^7$ points.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[regimes]
\Cref{thm:N1} uses nothing about rungs or $\tau$, so it holds in Regions I, II, III, including $\tau=\sqrt3/2$, $\tau\le0$ and $\tau>1$. \Cref{thm:N2} needs $t>\sqrt3/2+\eps$ with $\eps>0$ fixed; in Region~III with $\tau\ge\sqrt3/2+\eta$, $0<\eta\le1/50$, it holds with $\eps_0=\arctan(\eta/2)$, but then the exceptional count grows like $1/\eta$ and is not uniform as $\eta\to0$. For $\tau\le\sqrt3/2$ we only have \cref{thm:N1}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[local sharpness of \cref{thm:N1}]\label{rem:sharp}
Consider a single disc, $K=\Dd(w,\Dt)$ near $y$, with $|y-w|=\Dt-h$. A neighbour at angle $\psi$ from the outward radial direction satisfies $|q-w|^2=(\Dt-h)^2+1+2(\Dt-h)\cos\psi$. So $q$ lies on $\partial K$ if and only if $\cos\psi=c_K(h):=h+(h^2-1)/(2(\Dt-h))$, and $\dist(q,K)\ge t$ if and only if $\cos\psi\ge c_D(h):=h+t+((h+t)^2-1)/(2(\Dt-h))$; moreover $c_D(0)=\tau$ and $c_D$ is increasing in $h$.\chk{N} Two such D-feasible neighbours $60^\circ$ apart need $c_D(h)\le\cos30^\circ$, so this local picture requires $\tau<\sqrt3/2$. It does occur for $\tau$ just below $\sqrt3/2$: with $w=0$ and $\Dt=10^4$, take on $\Cc(w,\Dt)$ chords $[p_-,p_+]$ of length $2$ (every other chord position), let $y$ be the chord midpoint, $e:=(p_+-p_-)/2$, $\nu:=y/|y|$, $z_\pm:=y\pm e/2+(\sqrt3/2)\nu$ and $a_\pm:=y\pm e/2-(\sqrt3/2)\nu$. In $60$-digit decimal arithmetic (script \texttt{r4\_nb\_exact.py}) one checks that $|yp_\pm|=|yz_\pm|=|ya_\pm|=1$, all pairwise distances are at least $1$, the $z_\pm$ lie on $\Cc(w,\Dt+t)$ with $t=0.86598790\ldots$ and $\tau=\sqrt3/2-5.0\cdot10^{-5}$, $\dist(z_\pm,\Dd(w,\Dt))=t$, and $a_\pm\in\operatorname{int}\Dd(w,\Dt)$. So every such $y$ has degree $6$, two neighbours on $\partial K$ and two D-feasible neighbours, i.e.\ $\deg y+m_y=10$ as soon as $z_\pm\in S$. In this one-centre configuration the $z_\pm$ have no $\Dt$-partner; whether $\Theta(n)$ such points $y$ with $z_\pm\in S$ can occur in a global configuration is open. So local arguments of the kind above cannot improve the \emph{pointwise} constant $10$. They do improve the average: by \cref{lem:pin}, this configuration at a good point $y$ (in the sense of \cref{sec:N3}) whose neighbours $z_\pm$ are diametral endpoints forces $t\in[0.75,0.95]$, as in the example, and \cref{thm:N3} shows that the excess of such points over $8$ can be absorbed by their lower neighbours $a_\pm$, which have $\deg+m\le7$.
\end{remark}
\section{The degenerate regime \texorpdfstring{$\Delta>1.94\Dt$}{Delta > 1.94 Delta2}}\label{sec:degenerate}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:degenerate}
If $\Delta>1.94\,\Dt$ and $n\ge4$, then $\mult(\Dt)\le n+1$.
\end{proposition}

Put $\kappa:=(\Dt/\Delta)^2<1/1.94^2$. Note that this section does not use $\delta$.

\emph{Step 1: no two disjoint diametral pairs.} Suppose $(x,x')$ and $(z,z')$ are disjoint diametral pairs with midpoints $m,m'$. The four cross distances satisfy $\sum|ab|^2=2\Delta^2+4|m-m'|^2\ge2\Delta^2$, and each is either at most $\Dt$ or equal to $\Delta$. If at most one equals $\Delta$, the sum is at most $\Delta^2(1+3\kappa)<2\Delta^2$,\chk{C10} a contradiction. So at least two cross distances equal $\Delta$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item If two disjoint cross pairs equal $\Delta$, say $|xz|=|x'z'|=\Delta$, then $z$ and $x'$ are the two points of $\Cc(x,\Delta)\cap\Cc(z',\Delta)$, so $x,z,z',x'$ form a rhombus of side $\Delta$ whose diagonals satisfy $d_1^2+d_2^2=4\Delta^2$. One diagonal is at least $\sqrt2\Delta$, a contradiction.
\item If two cross pairs sharing a point equal $\Delta$, say $|xz|=|xz'|=\Delta$, then $xzz'$ is an equilateral triangle of side $\Delta$, and $x'\in\Cc(x,\Delta)$ with $|x'z|,|x'z'|\le\Delta$ lies on the $60^\circ$ arc of $\Cc(x,\Delta)$ between $z$ and $z'$, strictly inside it. One of $|x'z|,|x'z'|$ is at least $2\Delta\sin15^\circ$, and $(2\sin15^\circ)^2=2-\sqrt3>\kappa$,\chk{C10} so that distance lies in $(\Dt,\Delta)$, which is impossible.
\end{itemize}

\emph{Step 2: the diameter graph is a star.} A graph with no two disjoint edges is a star or a triangle. A triangle is impossible for $n\ge4$: a fourth point is not diametral, so it is within $\Dt$ of all three vertices, but $\Dt^2<\Delta^2/3$ is less than the squared circumradius.\chk{C10} So $D=\{y\}\cup A$ with $A$ the set of diametral partners of $y$; put $B:=X\setminus D\subseteq K$ and $W:=\{y\}\cup A$.

\emph{Step 3: where the $\Dt$-pairs are.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Within $B$: none.} For $a\in A$, $K\subseteq\Dd(y,\Dt)\cap\Dd(a,\Dt)$, a lens whose bounding box has squared diagonal less than $0.24\,\Dt^2$.\chk{C10} So $\operatorname{diam}K<\Dt$.
\item \emph{Within $A$: at most one.} $A\subseteq\Cc(y,\Delta)$, and its pairwise distances are at most $\Dt$ (a $\Delta$-pair inside $A$ would create a triangle in the diameter graph). A chord of $\Cc(y,\Delta)$ of length at most $\Dt<\Delta/1.94$ subtends an angle less than $30^\circ$ at $y$. So $A$ lies in an arc of angular width less than $60^\circ$, on which chord length is increasing in angular separation; a $\Dt$-pair in $A$ must realise the largest pairwise distance, hence be the two extreme points of $A$.
\item \emph{Between $y$ and $A$: none}, as those distances equal $\Delta$.
\end{itemize}
Hence $\mult(\Dt)\le1+I$, where $I:=\#\{(b,w):b\in B,\ w\in W,\ |bw|=\Dt\}$.

\emph{Step 4: $I\le|B|+|W|$.} We may assume $K$ has nonempty interior: an open chord between two points of $K$ lies in the interior of every disc $\Dd(w,\Dt)$, so otherwise $|B|\le|K\cap X|\le1$ and the bound is trivial. For $w\in X$ let $J_w:=\Cc(w,\Dt)\cap K$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Each $J_w$ is a single closed arc (possibly a point).} For $x\in X\setminus\{w\}$, $\Cc(w,\Dt)\cap\Dd(x,\Dt)$ is the closed arc $\{\cos(\theta-\theta_x)\ge|wx|/(2\Dt)\}$, of angular length at most $\pi$. For $p,q\in J_w$ we have $|pq|<\Dt<2\Dt$, so $p,q$ are not antipodal and the minor arc of $\Cc(w,\Dt)$ from $p$ to $q$ lies in every $\Dd(x,\Dt)$, hence in $K$. Since $\operatorname{diam}K<\Dt$, the angular spread of $J_w$ is less than $60^\circ$, so $J_w$ is a single closed arc.
\item \emph{No point is interior to two arcs.} Suppose $b$ is a relative-interior point of a nondegenerate arc $J_w$ and $b\in\Cc(w',\Dt)$ with $w'\in X\setminus\{w\}$. The function $f(\theta)=|w+\Dt e(\theta)-w'|^2-\Dt^2=|w-w'|^2+2\Dt\,e(\theta)\cdot(w-w')$ is $\le0$ on $J_w$ (as $K\subseteq\Dd(w',\Dt)$) and vanishes at $b$, so it has an interior local maximum with value $0$. But every local maximum of this shifted cosine is its global maximum $|w-w'|^2+2\Dt|w-w'|>0$, a contradiction.
\item \emph{Consequences.} Each nondegenerate $J_w$ is a subarc of $\partial K$, and distinct nondegenerate arcs have disjoint relative interiors. So a point $b\in B$ lies on at most two nondegenerate $W$-arcs (one on each side); any further incidences of $b$ come from degenerate arcs $J_w=\{b\}$.
\item \emph{Counting.} Let $I_{\mathrm{nd}}$ be the number of incidences from nondegenerate arcs. Then $I_{\mathrm{nd}}\le|B|+\#\{b:\text{two $W$-arcs meet at }b\}$. Mapping each such $b$ to the $W$-arc that starts at $b$ in the clockwise direction is injective, so $I_{\mathrm{nd}}\le|B|+\#\{w:J_w\text{ nondegenerate}\}$. Each degenerate arc contributes at most one incidence. Hence $I\le|B|+|W|$.
\end{itemize}
Therefore $\mult(\Dt)\le1+|B|+|A|+1=n+1$, proving \cref{prop:degenerate}.


\section{Assembly: the formalised bound \texorpdfstring{$15/11$}{15/11}}\label{sec:assembly}

The results of \cref{sec:reductions,sec:bad,sec:regions,sec:Rpoints,sec:degenerate} already give the bound of the previous version of this paper, which is the one formalised in Lean (\cref{sec:sat}). Theorem~1 is proved in \cref{sec:N3,sec:P4} by adding one estimate to this argument.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:main15}
There is an absolute constant $C_0$ ($C_0=3N_0+2\cdot10^8$ suffices) such that every $n$-point set $X\subset\R^2$ satisfies
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le\tfrac{15}{11}\,n+C_0$;
\item if moreover $\Delta\le1.94\,\Dt$ and $\tau(X)\ge\sqrt3/2+3/100$, then $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le\tfrac43\,n+C_0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
Part (b) is superseded by Theorem~1(a); we keep it because its proof is pointwise (\cref{thm:N2}).

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
regime ($\delta=1$, $\beta=1/100$) & $e(S)\le$ & $\deg y+m_y\le$ & $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le$\\
\midrule
degenerate: $\Delta>1.94\Dt$, $n\ge4$ & --- & --- & $n+1$ (\cref{prop:degenerate})\\
nondegenerate, $\Dt<10^4$ & --- & --- & $3n<3N_0$ (\cref{sec:reductions})\\
I: $\tau\le\sqrt3/2-2\beta$ (incl.\ $\tau\le0$) & $s+C$ & $10$ (\cref{thm:N1}) & $\frac{15}{11}n+C$\\
III: $\sqrt3/2-2\beta<\tau<\sqrt3/2+3\beta$ & $s+p+C$ & $10$ (\cref{thm:N1}) & $\frac{15}{11}n+C$\\
II: $\sqrt3/2+3\beta\le\tau\le1$ & $s+p+C$ & $8$ (\cref{thm:N2}) & $\frac43n+C$\\
II: $\tau>1$ ($\Leftrightarrow t>1$) & $s+C$ & $8$ (\cref{thm:N2}) & $\frac43n+C$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The regimes in the proof of \cref{thm:main15}. In the last column $C<2\cdot10^8$; the $R$-bounds hold for all but fewer than $7.1\cdot10^7$ points of $R$.}
\end{table}

The $\tau$-intervals partition $\R$, the split at $\Delta=1.94\,\Dt$ is exhaustive, and the cases $n\le3$ (or $X$ with a single distance) are trivial.

\emph{Constants.} By \cref{prop:bad}, $C_{\mathrm{bad}}<1.83\cdot10^7$. By \cref{sec:regions}, in every $\tau$-regime of the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt\ge10^4$,
\[
e(S)\le s+p+C_e,\qquad C_e:=2C_{\mathrm{bad}}+81<3.7\cdot10^7
\]
(Region~I and $\tau>1$: $C_{\mathrm{bad}}$; Region~II: $C_{\mathrm{bad}}+5$; Region~III: $C_{\mathrm{bad}}+6$ for $\tau\neq\sqrt3/2$ and $2C_{\mathrm{bad}}+81$ for $\tau=\sqrt3/2$). By \cref{thm:N1}, $\deg y+m_y\le10$ for all but $C'<7.1\cdot10^7$ points $y\in R$, and in Region~II, by \cref{thm:N2}, $\deg y+m_y\le 8$ for all but $C'<7.1\cdot10^7$ points. \Cref{lem:lpnew} gives
\begin{align*}
\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}&\le\tfrac{15}{11}n+C_e+C'\quad\text{in general, and}\\
\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}&\le\tfrac43n+C_e+C'\quad\text{in Region II,}
\end{align*}
and $C_e+C'<1.1\cdot10^8<2\cdot10^8$. The degenerate regime gives $n+1\le\frac43n+1$, and the small cases give at most $3N_0$. So \cref{thm:main15} holds with $C_0=3N_0+2\cdot10^8$. Part (b) is the Region~II row: its hypotheses are $\Delta\le1.94\,\Dt$ and $\tau\ge\sqrt3/2+3\beta$, and if $\Dt<10^4$ the small-case bound applies. \qed

\begin{remark}\label{rem:bottleneck}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em,label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{The bottleneck of \cref{thm:main15} is the $R$-term.} The estimate $e(S)\le s+p+O(1)$ is sharp enough for $\frac43$ (\cref{rem:lpvalues}); the value $\frac{15}{11}$ comes only from the pointwise bound $\deg y+m_y\le10$ of \cref{thm:N1} in Regions I and III. By \cref{rem:sharp}, this pointwise bound cannot be improved by local arguments. \Cref{sec:N3} replaces it by the average bound $8$, which removes this bottleneck.
\item \emph{What remains for $9/7$.} If the average of $\deg y+m_y$ over $R$ were $6+o(1)$, Region~I would give $\frac97$, but with the $p$-term of \cref{sec:regions} Regions II and III would still give only $\frac43$ (\cref{rem:lpvalues}). So reaching $\frac97$ with this method also requires sharpening the rung count; in Region~II this is done in \cref{sec:II} (the $p$-term is halved, which allows $\frac54$), and Region~III is discussed in \cref{sec:open}; for $\tau>1$, $\frac97$ is reached by a different argument (\cref{sec:Eprime}).
\item \emph{Consistency with the lower bound.} The construction of ienjoymath~\cite{ienjoymath} for $L\ge9/7$ lies in Region~I ($t/\delta\to0$). In it $e(S)=|S|$, every point of $S$ has $\Dt$-degree $2$ or $4$ (so $Q=\emptyset$ and $\mult(\Dt)=\frac32|S|$), and every point of $R$ has $m_y=0$ and $\deg y+m_y\le6$; we checked the last statement numerically for $n=60,120,250$ (\cref{app:verification}). This is consistent with \cref{thm:N1}, which is only an upper bound; in Region~I the gap between $\frac97$ and the bounds $\frac{15}{11}$, $\frac43$, $\frac{21}{16}$ is exactly the gap between the average $6$ and the averages $10$, $8$, $7$ in the $R$-term.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}
\section{The average weight of \texorpdfstring{$R$}{R}: proof of Theorem 1(a)}\label{sec:N3}

For $y\in R$ we call $v(y):=\deg y+m_y$ the \emph{weight} of $y$. By \cref{lem:identity}, $\mult(\delta)=e(S)+\frac12\sum_{y\in R}v(y)$, and $v(y)\le12$. \Cref{thm:N1} gives $v(y)\le10$ for all but $O(1)$ points. In this section we prove that the average weight is at most $8$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:N3}
In the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt\ge10^4$,
\[
\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\ \le\ 8r+C_3,\qquad C_3:=1.1\cdot10^9 .
\]
\end{theorem}
No assumption on $\tau$ is made. \Cref{thm:N3} is formalised in Lean (\cref{sec:sat}), and it has been through two independent referee passes; both found it correct, and their cosmetic corrections are incorporated below. The proof has two ideas. First, a point of weight at least $9$ at which $\partial K$ is flat has one of four local shapes, and in each shape a K-row neighbour and a diametral neighbour have a height difference that \cref{lem:identities}(b) forces to be within $0.05$ of $t$ (\cref{lem:pin}); so the shape of the heavy points is determined by $t$. Second, every such point has one or two neighbours deeper inside $K$ that have weight at most $7$ and belong to no other heavy point; each heavy point passes its excess over $8$ to them.

\subsection{Two lemmas with parameters}
The proofs of \cref{lem:thinK,lem:BT,lem:flat} work with other parameters. For $\Lambda\ge1$ and $y\in R\setminus D$ with $h\le1$, if the perimeter of $K$ is at least $2\Lambda$, let $\gamma_y^\Lambda$ be the closed arc of $\partial K$ of points at arc-length distance at most $\Lambda$ from $p_0$; so $\gamma_y=\gamma_y^{232}$.

\begin{lemma}[packing in thin $K$]\label{lem:Tstar}
Let $1\le\ell\le\Dt/100$ and $\Lambda>115\,\ell$. Suppose $p,b\in\partial K$ satisfy $|pb|\le\ell$, and both arcs of $\partial K$ between $p$ and $b$ have length greater than $\Lambda$. Then $K$ lies in a rectangle of length less than $250\ell$ and width less than $5.4\ell$, and hence $|X\setminus D|<(250\ell+1)(5.4\ell+1)\cdot4/\pi$. For $\ell=4$ this is less than $3\cdot10^4$, and for $\ell=7$ less than $10^5$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We follow the proof of \cref{lem:thinK}, with $p$ in the role of both $p_0$ and $y$. Let $\nu_p,\nu_b$ be outer normals of $K$ at $p,b$, with supporting half-planes $H_p,H_b\supseteq K$ and boundary lines $\ell_p,\ell_b$. If $\nu_b=\nu_p$, then $\ell_p=\ell_b$ as in the proof of \cref{lem:thinK}, and $[p,b]\subseteq K\cap\ell_p\subseteq\partial K$ is a boundary arc of length at most $\ell<\Lambda$, a contradiction. If $\nu_b\neq\pm\nu_p$, then $Z:=H_p\cap H_b$ is a cone with apex $A$ and opening $\theta\in(0,\pi)$, and $p,b$ lie on its two boundary rays. As in the proof of \cref{lem:thinK}, either $[p,b]\subseteq\partial K$ (a contradiction as before) or one of the two arcs from $p$ to $b$ has length at most $|Ap|+|Ab|$. In the triangle $Apb$ the law of sines gives $|Ap|+|Ab|=|pb|(\sin\angle p+\sin\angle b)/\sin\theta\le|pb|\cdot2\cos(\theta/2)/\sin\theta=|pb|/\sin(\theta/2)$ (and $|Ap|+|Ab|=|pb|$ if $A\in\{p,b\}$). For $\theta\ge1^\circ$ this is at most $\ell/\sin(0.5^\circ)<114.6\,\ell<\Lambda$, a contradiction. So $\theta<1^\circ$, or $\nu_b=-\nu_p$ (a strip; put $\theta:=0$).

Now apply the fatness step of the proof of \cref{lem:thinK} with $p$ in place of $y$. The cross-section of $Z$ at the level of $p$ has length $w_0=\dist(p,\ell_b)/\cos(\theta/2)\le\ell/\cos(0.5^\circ)<1.0001\,\ell$, since $p\in\ell_p$. With $L$ and $W$ as there, $W\le w_0+2\tan(\theta/2)L$ and $L\le2\Dt$. If $L\ge250\ell$, then $\tan\varphi^*=2W/L\le2w_0/(250\ell)+4\tan(0.5^\circ)<0.0430$, and
\[
\frac{2\Dt\cos\varphi^*-W}{\Dt}\ \ge\ 2\Bigl(1-\frac{\tan^2\varphi^*}{2}\Bigr)-4\tan(0.5^\circ)-\frac{w_0}{\Dt}\ >\ 1.998-0.035-0.011\ >\ 1.94,
\]
which contradicts $|w_{\mathrm{bot}}-w_{\mathrm{top}}|\le\Delta\le1.94\,\Dt$ exactly as in that proof. Hence $L<250\ell$ and $W<1.0001\,\ell+500\ell\tan(0.5^\circ)<5.4\,\ell$. The disjoint discs of radius $\frac12$ about the points of $X\setminus D\subseteq K$ lie in a rectangle of area less than $(250\ell+1)(5.4\ell+1)$.\chk{N3}
\end{proof}

The proof of \cref{lem:BT}, with $232$ replaced by $\Lambda$, gives the following: \emph{the number of $y\in R\setminus D$ with $h\le1$ and $\turn(\gamma_y^\Lambda)>\eps_0$ is less than $2\pi(2\Lambda+3)^2/\eps_0$.} (If $b\in\gamma^\Lambda_y$ then $|b-y|\le\Lambda+1$, and at most $(2\Lambda+3)^2$ points of $R$ lie within that distance of $b$.)

\begin{lemma}[frame]\label{lem:frame}
Let $\Lambda\ge100$, $1\le\rho\le\Lambda/2$, $0<\eps_0\le1/50$ with $\Lambda(1-\cos\eps_0)<1$, and $\eps:=\tan\eps_0$. Let $y\in R\setminus D$ with $h\le1$, and suppose that the perimeter of $K$ is at least $2\Lambda$, that $\turn(\gamma^\Lambda_y)\le\eps_0$, and that $\partial K\cap\Dd(y,\rho)\subseteq\gamma^\Lambda_y$. Use the coordinates of \cref{lem:flat} (origin $p_0$, first axis $t_0$, second axis $n_0$, so $y=(0,-h)$), and write $q=(q_x,q_z)$. Then there is a concave function $f$ on $[-(\Lambda-1),\Lambda-1]$ with $f(0)=0$ and $-\eps|x|\le f(x)\le0$, whose graph is contained in $\gamma^\Lambda_y$ and contains $\partial K\cap\Dd(y,\rho)$, and $U_\rho:=\Dd(y,\rho)\cap\{z<f(x)\}\subseteq\operatorname{int}K$. For every $q\in X\cap\Dd(y,\rho)$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(G\arabic*)},leftmargin=3em]
\item if $q\notin\operatorname{int}K$, then $q_z\ge f(q_x)$;
\item if $q\in S\setminus D$, then $q_z=f(q_x)$;
\item if $q\in D$, then $q_z\ge f(q_x)+t$;
\item if $q\in\operatorname{int}K$, then $q_z<f(q_x)$.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(G\arabic*)},leftmargin=3em,resume]
\item every outer normal of $K$ at a point of $\gamma^\Lambda_y$ makes an angle at most $\eps_0$ with $n_0$. In particular, if $y'$ also satisfies the hypotheses and $|y-y'|\le\rho-1$, then the vectors $n_0$ of $y$ and $y'$ make an angle at most $\eps_0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the proof of \cref{lem:flat} with $232$, $231$ and $\Dd(y,1)$ replaced by $\Lambda$, $\Lambda-1$ and $\Dd(y,\rho)$: the hypothesis $\Lambda(1-\cos\eps_0)<1$ gives $\Lambda\cos\eps_0>\Lambda-1$, and the $x$-range $[-\rho,\rho]$ of $\Dd(y,\rho)$ lies inside $[-(\Lambda-1),\Lambda-1]$. (G1), (G2) and (G4) are proved as (F1), (F2) and (F4). For (G3): if $q\in D$ had $q_z<f(q_x)$, then $q\in U_\rho\subseteq K$, whereas $D\cap K=\emptyset$ by \cref{lem:ballpoly}(c); and the graph point below $q$ lies in $K$, so $t\le\dist(q,K)\le q_z-f(q_x)$. (G5): the normal set $N(\gamma^\Lambda_y)$ is an arc of length at most $\eps_0$ containing $n_0$. If $y'$ is as stated, its nearest boundary point $p_0'$ satisfies $|p_0'-y|\le1+|y-y'|\le\rho$, so $p_0'\in\partial K\cap\Dd(y,\rho)\subseteq\gamma^\Lambda_y$, and the vector $n_0$ of $y'$ is an outer normal of $K$ at $p_0'$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{lem:flat} is essentially the case $\Lambda=232$, $\rho=1$. In the situation of \cref{lem:frame} we write a $\delta$-neighbour $q$ of $y$ as $q=y+(\sin\psi,\cos\psi)$ and call $c(q):=\cos\psi=q_z+h$ its \emph{height}. By (G1)--(G4) and \cref{sec:structR}(b), every neighbour of $y$ is of exactly one of three kinds:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item a \emph{K-neighbour} ($q\in S\setminus D$): $c(q)=h+f(q_x)\in[h-\eps,h]$, and $|\psi|<\pi/2+\eps_0$ as in (F1);
\item a \emph{D-neighbour} ($q\in D$, in $S$ or in $R$): $c(q)\ge h+t-\eps$;
\item an \emph{interior neighbour} ($q\in\operatorname{int}K$, hence $q\in R\setminus D$): $c(q)<h$.
\end{itemize}
The neighbours of $y$ in $S$ are its K-neighbours and its D-neighbours in $S$. As in the proof of \cref{thm:N1}, $m_y\le4$.

\begin{lemma}[diametral points are nearly level]\label{lem:DN}
In the situation of \cref{lem:frame}, let $z\in D\cap\Dd(y,\rho)$ with $|z_x|\le\xi$ and $z_z\le\zeta$, where $\xi,\zeta\le\rho$. Let $z^*$ be a diametral partner of $z$ and $u:=(z^*-z)/\Delta=(a_1,a_2)$. Then $a_2<0$ and $|a_1|<\kappa_{\mathrm D}|a_2|$ with $\kappa_{\mathrm D}:=((\Lambda-1)\eps+\zeta)/(\Lambda-1-\xi)$. Consequently, any two points $z,z'\in D\cap\Dd(y,\rho)$ satisfying these bounds have $|z_z-z'_z|<\kappa_{\mathrm D}\,|z_x-z'_x|$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every $k\in K$ has $|k-z^*|\le\Dt<\Delta=|z-z^*|$, so $(k-z)\cdot u>|k-z|^2/(2\Delta)\ge0$. The graph point $g$ directly below $z$ lies in $K$ and strictly below $z$ by (G3), and $(g-z)\cdot u=(f(z_x)-z_z)a_2>0$ gives $a_2<0$. Put $c:=-a_2>0$. The graph points $g_\pm:=(\pm(\Lambda-1),f(\pm(\Lambda-1)))\in K$ give $(g_\pm-z)\cdot u>0$, i.e.\ $(\pm(\Lambda-1)-z_x)\,a_1>(f(\pm(\Lambda-1))-z_z)\,c\ge-((\Lambda-1)\eps+z_z)\,c$, where $(\Lambda-1)\eps+z_z>0$ by (G3). The sign $+$ gives $a_1>-c\,((\Lambda-1)\eps+\zeta)/(\Lambda-1-\xi)$ and the sign $-$ gives $a_1<c\,((\Lambda-1)\eps+\zeta)/(\Lambda-1-\xi)$. For the consequence, apply \cref{lem:identities}(a), which holds for every point of $D$, to $z$ and to $z'$: $(z'-z)\cdot u_z>0$ and $(z-z')\cdot u_{z'}>0$ give $z'_z-z_z<\kappa_{\mathrm D}|z'_x-z_x|$ and $z_z-z'_z<\kappa_{\mathrm D}|z'_x-z_x|$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Good points}
In the rest of this section $\eps_0=1/50$, $\eps=\tan\eps_0<0.02001$, $\Lambda=464$ and $\rho=3$; then $\Lambda(1-\cos\eps_0)<0.1$. A point $y\in R\setminus D$ with $h\le1$ is \emph{good} if the perimeter of $K$ is at least $928$, $\turn(\gamma^{464}_y)\le\eps_0$, and $\partial K\cap\Dd(y,3)\subseteq\gamma^{464}_y$. At a good point \cref{lem:frame} applies with $\Lambda=464$, $\rho=3$, and \cref{lem:DN} gives the constant $\kappa_{\mathrm D}<(463\eps+3)/460<0.0267$ on $D\cap\Dd(y,3)$, and $\kappa_{\mathrm D}<(463\eps+1)/462<0.0224$ for D-neighbours of $y$ (for which $|z_x|\le1$ and $z_z\le1-h\le1$).\chk{N3} By (G5), two good points $y,y'$ with $|y-y'|\le2$ have frames whose axes differ by at most $\eps_0$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:fewbad}
Fewer than $N_{\mathrm g}:=2.74\cdot10^8$ points $y\in R$ with $v(y)\ge7$ are not good.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Points of $R\cap D$ and points of $R\setminus D$ with $h>1$ have $v(y)\le6$ (\cref{sec:structR}(a),(c)). If the perimeter of $K$ is less than $928$, then $\operatorname{diam}K<464$ and $|X\setminus D|\le(2\cdot464+1)^2<8.7\cdot10^5$. The points with $\turn(\gamma^{464}_y)>\eps_0$ number less than $2\pi\cdot931^2\cdot50<2.73\cdot10^8$. If some $b\in\partial K\cap\Dd(y,3)$ is not on $\gamma^{464}_y$, then $|p_0b|\le4$ and both arcs from $p_0$ to $b$ are longer than $464>115\cdot4$, so \cref{lem:Tstar} with $\ell=4$ gives $|X\setminus D|<3\cdot10^4$.\chk{N3}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Heavy points}
A point $y\in R$ is \emph{heavy} if $v(y)\ge9$. Neighbours of $y$ are pairwise at least $60^\circ$ apart as seen from $y$.

\begin{lemma}[pairs]\label{lem:pairs}
Let $y$ be good. If two K-neighbours of $y$ are at $\psi_1<\psi_2$, then $|\sin\frac{\psi_1+\psi_2}2|\le\eps$, so $|\psi_1+\psi_2|<2.30^\circ$. If two D-neighbours (in $S$ or not) are at $\psi_1<\psi_2$, then $|\sin\frac{\psi_1+\psi_2}2|<0.0448$, so $|\psi_1+\psi_2|<5.62^\circ$. Consequently $y$ has at most two K-neighbours and at most two D-neighbours.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
K- and D-neighbours have $|\psi|<90^\circ+\eps_0$ (D-neighbours have $c>0$), so $\psi_2-\psi_1\in[60^\circ,180^\circ+2\eps_0]$ and $\sin\frac{\psi_2-\psi_1}2\ge\frac12$. Since $|c_1-c_2|=2|\sin\frac{\psi_1+\psi_2}2|\sin\frac{\psi_2-\psi_1}2$, we get $|\sin\frac{\psi_1+\psi_2}2|\le|c_1-c_2|$. For K-neighbours $|c_1-c_2|\le\eps<\sin1.15^\circ$. For D-neighbours, \cref{lem:DN} gives $|c_1-c_2|<0.0224\cdot2<\sin2.81^\circ$. Three neighbours of one kind would satisfy $\psi_1\approx-\psi_2\approx\psi_3$ up to $2\cdot5.62^\circ<60^\circ$, which is impossible.\chk{N3}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[classification]\label{lem:heavy}
Let $y$ be good and heavy. Then $(\deg y,m_y)\in\{(6,4),(5,4),(6,3)\}$, and $y$ is of one of the following types (angles are values of $\psi$).
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=3.2em]
\item[\textup{(F6)}] $(6,4)$: K-neighbours within $\eps_0$ of $\pm90^\circ$, D-neighbours in $S$ within $\eps_0$ of $\pm30^\circ$, and interior neighbours within $\eps_0$ of $\pm150^\circ$; $v(y)=10$.
\item[\textup{(F5)}] $(5,4)$: the same four neighbours in $S$, and one interior neighbour with $\psi\in(150^\circ-\eps_0,210^\circ+\eps_0)$; $v(y)=9$.
\item[\textup{(HF)}] $(6,3)$: the six neighbours form a regular hexagon within $2.81^\circ$ of $\{\pm30^\circ,\pm90^\circ,\pm150^\circ\}$; the neighbours in $S$ are K-neighbours near $\pm90^\circ$ and one D-neighbour near $+30^\circ$ or $-30^\circ$ (HF1), or D-neighbours near $\pm30^\circ$ and one K-neighbour near $+90^\circ$ or $-90^\circ$ (HF2); the neighbours near $\pm150^\circ$ are interior; $v(y)=9$.
\item[\textup{(cap)}] $(6,3)$: the six neighbours form a regular hexagon within $1.15^\circ$ of $\{0^\circ,\pm60^\circ,\pm120^\circ,180^\circ\}$; K-neighbours near $\pm60^\circ$, a D-neighbour in $S$ near $0^\circ$, interior neighbours near $\pm120^\circ$ and $180^\circ$; $v(y)=9$.
\end{enumerate}
We call F6, F5 and HF \emph{fans}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Since $m_y\le4$ and $m_y\le\deg y\le6$, $v\ge9$ leaves exactly the three pairs listed. A neighbour with $|\psi|>90^\circ+\eps_0$ is neither a K-neighbour nor (having $c<0<h+t-\eps$) a D-neighbour, so it is interior.

\emph{$m_y=4$.} The four neighbours in $S$ lie in the open arc $|\psi|<90^\circ+\eps_0$ and are pairwise at least $60^\circ$ apart, so they are within $\eps_0$ of $-90^\circ,-30^\circ,30^\circ,90^\circ$. By \cref{lem:pairs} two are K- and two are D-neighbours, and each pair of the same kind is mirror-symmetric up to $5.62^\circ$; so the pairs are $\{\pm90^\circ\}$ and $\{\pm30^\circ\}$. If the K-neighbours were near $\pm30^\circ$, then $h\ge\cos31.15^\circ>0.85$, while a D-neighbour near $\pm90^\circ$ has $c\le\sin\eps_0<0.02<h+t-\eps$; impossible. This gives the neighbours in $S$ of F6 and F5. For $\deg y=6$ the six neighbours are exactly $60^\circ$ apart, so the remaining two are within $\eps_0$ of $\pm150^\circ$. For $\deg y=5$ the fifth neighbour is at least $60^\circ$ from both K-neighbours, so $\psi\in(150^\circ-\eps_0,210^\circ+\eps_0)$.

\emph{$(6,3)$.} The six neighbours form a regular hexagon $\{\varphi+60^\circ k\}$. By \cref{lem:pairs}, two of the three neighbours in $S$ are of the same kind and form a mirror pair up to $5.62^\circ$ ($2.30^\circ$ for a K-pair); so $\varphi$ is within $2.81^\circ$ ($1.15^\circ$) of a multiple of $30^\circ$. Neighbours in $S$ have $|\psi|<90^\circ+\eps_0$, so they lie near $0^\circ,\pm60^\circ$ in the first case and near $\pm30^\circ,\pm90^\circ$ in the second. The mirror pair is $\{\pm60^\circ\}$, $\{\pm30^\circ\}$ or $\{\pm90^\circ\}$, and the third neighbour in $S$ is of the other kind. This leaves six cases.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item K near $\pm60^\circ$, D near $0^\circ$: the cap.
\item D near $\pm60^\circ$, K near $0^\circ$: the K-neighbour gives $h\ge\cos2.81^\circ>0.998$, so a D-neighbour needs $c\ge h+t-\eps>0.978$; but the D-neighbours have $c\le\cos57.19^\circ<0.55$. Impossible.
\item K near $\pm30^\circ$, D near $\pm90^\circ$: $h\ge\cos31.15^\circ>0.85$; the D-neighbour has $c\le\sin2.81^\circ<0.05$ but needs $c\ge h+t-\eps>0.82$. Impossible.
\item D near $\pm30^\circ$, K near $+90^\circ$ or $-90^\circ$: HF2.
\item K near $\pm90^\circ$, D near $+30^\circ$ or $-30^\circ$: HF1.
\item D near $\pm90^\circ$, K near $\pm30^\circ$: the D-neighbours give $h\le c-t+\eps\le\sin2.81^\circ+\eps<0.07$, the K-neighbours give $h\ge\cos32.81^\circ>0.84$. Impossible.
\end{itemize}
In HF and in the cap, the neighbours near $\pm150^\circ$, resp.\ $\pm120^\circ$ and $180^\circ$, have $|\psi|>90^\circ+\eps_0$ and are interior.\chk{N3}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[$t$ is pinned]\label{lem:pin}
If some good point is a fan, then $t\in[0.75,0.95]$. If some good point is a cap, then $t\in[0.44,0.56]$. In particular, fans and caps do not both occur in one set $X$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $y$ be a good fan or cap. By \cref{lem:heavy}, $y$ has a K-neighbour $k$ and a D-neighbour $z\in S$ whose heights differ by $\Delta c:=c(z)-c(k)$ with
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item $\Delta c\in[\cos32.81^\circ-\sin2.81^\circ,\ \cos27.19^\circ+\eps]\subseteq[0.7914,0.9096]$ for a fan (using $c(k)\ge h-\eps\ge-\eps$), and
\item $\Delta c\in[\cos1.15^\circ-\cos58.85^\circ,\ 1-\cos61.15^\circ]\subseteq[0.4824,0.5175]$ for a cap.
\end{itemize}
Since $k\in\partial K\cap\Dd(y,1)\subseteq\gamma_y^{464}$, (G5) shows that $u_k=(w_k-k)/\Dt$, an inward normal at $k$, equals $(\sin\beta',-\cos\beta')$ with $|\beta'|\le\eps_0$. With $z-k=(\Delta x,\Delta c)$ and $|\Delta x|\le2$,
\[
(z-k)\cdot u_k=\Delta x\sin\beta'-\Delta c\cos\beta'\le2\sin\eps_0-0.48\cos\eps_0<0 .
\]
So $z$ is not normal for $k$, and by \cref{lem:identities}(b), $|z-w_k|=\Delta=\Dt+t$. Expanding $|z-w_k|^2=|z-k|^2-2\Dt(z-k)\cdot u_k+\Dt^2$ gives exactly
\[
(z-k)\cdot u_k=-t+\frac{d^2-t^2}{2\Dt},\qquad d:=|z-k|\le2 .
\]
(For $d=1$ this is \cref{lem:identities}(c).) Hence $t=\Delta c\cos\beta'-\Delta x\sin\beta'+(d^2-t^2)/(2\Dt)$; this gives $t<1$, and since $\Dt\ge10^4$,
\[
|t-\Delta c|\le\Delta c\,(1-\cos\eps_0)+2\sin\eps_0+2\cdot10^{-4}<0.0404 .
\]
This gives $t\in[0.751,0.950]$ for a fan and $t\in[0.442,0.558]$ for a cap.\chk{N3}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{lem:pin} is where the global structure enters: the local configuration of \cref{rem:sharp} is realisable only if $t$ is close to $\frac{\sqrt3}2$. In $60$-digit arithmetic the configuration of \cref{rem:sharp} has $t=0.86598\ldots$, and a chain of caps built on chords of length $\sqrt3$ of one circle has $t=0.49996\ldots$ (\texttt{r6\_n3\_examples.py}); both lie in the windows of \cref{lem:pin}. Outside $[0.44,0.56]\cup[0.75,0.95]$, all but fewer than $N_{\mathrm g}$ points of $R$ have $v(y)\le8$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Charging}
Each good heavy point $y$ sends one unit of charge to each of its \emph{receivers}: an F6 point to its two interior neighbours near $\pm150^\circ$; an F5 point to its fifth (interior) neighbour; an HF point to its interior neighbour near $+150^\circ$ (any fixed choice of one of the two works below); a cap to its interior neighbour near $180^\circ$. Other points send nothing.

\begin{lemma}[receivers]\label{lem:receivers}
Let $a$ be a receiver of a good heavy point $y$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $a\in R\setminus D$, and $a$ is not heavy: if $y$ is a fan, then $m_a\le1$ and $v(a)\le7$; if $y$ is a cap, then $m_a=0$ and $v(a)\le6$.
\item $a$ is a receiver of no other good heavy point.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) Receivers are interior neighbours, so they lie in $\operatorname{int}K$ and hence in $R\setminus D$. Let $y$ be a fan. The receiver has $\psi_a\in[147^\circ,213^\circ]$, so $a_z=\cos\psi_a-h\le-\cos33^\circ<-0.838$. A neighbour $q$ of $a$ in $S$ lies in $\Dd(y,2)$ and outside $\operatorname{int}K$, so $q_z\ge f(q_x)\ge-2\eps>-0.041$ by (G1). Hence $(q-a)_z>0.797$: as seen from $a$, $q$ lies within $37.2^\circ$ of the upward vertical. So does $y$, since $(y-a)_z=-\cos\psi_a\ge\cos33^\circ$; and $y\in R$. Three neighbours of $a$ pairwise at least $60^\circ$ apart do not fit into a window of width $74.4^\circ$, so $a$ has at most one neighbour in $S$, and $v(a)\le6+1$. Let $y$ be a cap. Then $h\ge c(k)\ge\cos61.15^\circ>0.48$ for its K-neighbours $k$, and the receiver $b$ has $\psi_b$ within $1.15^\circ$ of $180^\circ$, so $b_z<-1.48$. A neighbour $q\in S$ of $b$ would satisfy $q_z\ge-0.041$ and $q_z\le b_z+1<-0.48$; so $m_b=0$.

(b) Suppose $a$ receives from good heavy points $y\neq y'$. By \cref{lem:pin} both are fans or both are caps. Since $|y-y'|\le2$, their frames differ by at most $\eps_0$; we measure directions from $a$ in the frame of $y$. If both are caps, then as seen from $a$, $y$ lies within $1.15^\circ$ and $y'$ within $1.15^\circ+\eps_0<2.3^\circ$ of the upward vertical; but they are distinct neighbours of $a$, hence at least $60^\circ$ apart. Impossible. Let both be fans. The directions $\alpha,\alpha'$ of $y,y'$ from $a$ satisfy $|\alpha|\le33^\circ$, $|\alpha'|\le33^\circ+\eps_0<34.2^\circ$ and $|\alpha-\alpha'|\ge60^\circ$. Hence $|y'-y|=2\sin\frac{|\alpha'-\alpha|}2\in[1,1.107]$, and $y'-y$ makes an angle at most $\frac12|\alpha+\alpha'|\le4.2^\circ$ with the horizontal. Now $y$ has a neighbour $q$ in the direction within $2.81^\circ$ of $\pm90^\circ$ on the side of $y'$ (F6 and F5 have K-neighbours near $\pm90^\circ$, and HF points have six neighbours), and $|y'-q|\le0.107+2\sin3.51^\circ<0.25<1$.\chk{N3} Distinct points of $X$ are at least $1$ apart, so $y'=q$. If $y$ is F6, F5 or HF1, then $q\in S$, whereas $y'\in R$; impossible. So $y$ is HF2 and $y'$ is its neighbour near $+90^\circ$, say (the other case is symmetric), which is not in $S$; by the same argument with the roles exchanged, $y'$ is HF2 and $y$ is its neighbour near $-90^\circ$. The hexagons are regular, so the D-neighbour $z$ of $y$ near $+30^\circ$ and the D-neighbour $z'$ of $y'$ near $-30^\circ$ are both at distance $1$ from $y$ and from $y'$. Both lie above the line $yy'$ (their heights above $y$, resp.\ $y'$, exceed $0.8$, while $|y_z-y'_z|<0.09$), and only one point at distance $1$ from both $y$ and $y'$ lies on that side; hence $z=z'$. Then $z$ has four distinct neighbours: $y$, $y'$, the D-neighbour of $y$ near $-30^\circ$, and the D-neighbour of $y'$ near $+30^\circ$. But every point of $D$ has at most three neighbours (\cref{sec:structR}(a): by \cref{lem:identities}(a), $X\setminus\{z\}$ lies in an open half-plane bounded by a line through $z$). This contradiction proves (b).
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:N3}]
Give each $y\in R$ the charge $v(y)$ and apply the rule above; the total charge is unchanged. A good heavy point is not a receiver (receivers have $v\le7$), and it ends with charge $10-2$ (F6) or $9-1$ (F5, HF, cap), i.e.\ $8$. A receiver receives exactly one unit (\cref{lem:receivers}(b)) and ends with at most $7+1=8$. Every other point of $R$ keeps its weight, which is at most $8$ unless the point is heavy and not good; by \cref{lem:fewbad} there are fewer than $N_{\mathrm g}$ such points, and each has weight at most $12$ (\cref{lem:identity}). Hence $\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\le8r+4N_{\mathrm g}<8r+1.1\cdot10^9$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Theorem 1(a)}
If $n\le3$ or $X$ has only one distance there is nothing to prove. In the degenerate regime $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le n+1\le\frac43n+1$ (\cref{prop:degenerate}). In the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt<10^4$ the bound $3n<3N_0$ of \cref{sec:reductions} applies. In the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt\ge10^4$, \cref{sec:regions} gives $e(S)\le s+p+C_e$ with $C_e<3.7\cdot10^7$ in every $\tau$-regime (see \cref{sec:assembly}; the $p$-term is needed only in Regions II and III, \cref{sec:regionII,sec:regionIII}), and \cref{thm:N3} gives $\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\le8r+C_3$. \Cref{lem:lpavg}(a) yields
\[
\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le\tfrac43n+\tfrac13\bigl(C_e+\tfrac12C_3\bigr)<\tfrac43n+2\cdot10^8 .
\]
So Theorem~1(a) holds with $C_0=3N_0+2\cdot10^8$. \qed

\begin{remark}\label{rem:N3}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em,label=(\alph*)]
\item The two inputs of the proof are of different status: the bound $e(S)\le s+p+C$ in Regions II and III (\cref{sec:regionII,sec:regionIII}) is part of the formalised \cref{thm:main15}, and \cref{thm:N3} is formalised separately (\cref{sec:sat}); so Theorem~1(a) as a whole is formalised.
\item With the rung count of \cref{sec:regions}, the value $\frac43$ is the limit of the method in Regions II and III, whatever the average weight (\cref{rem:lpvalues}). In Region~I and for $\tau>1$ there is no $p$-term, and \cref{sec:P4} lowers the average weight to $7$; in Region~II, \cref{sec:II} halves the $p$-term and also lowers the average weight to $7$.
\item In the construction of ienjoymath~\cite{ienjoymath}, $t\to0$, so by \cref{lem:pin} there are no good heavy points; indeed every point of $R$ has weight at most $6$ there (\cref{rem:bottleneck}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Region I and \texorpdfstring{$\tau>1$}{tau > 1}: proof of Theorem 1(b)}\label{sec:P4}

In Region~I and for $\tau>1$ there is no $p$-term ($e(S)\le s+C_{\mathrm{bad}}$, \cref{sec:regionI,sec:common}), so every improvement of the average weight improves the bound (\cref{rem:lpvalues}). We lower the average from $8$ to $7$. Region~II, where a $p$-term is present, is treated in \cref{sec:II}.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:P4}
Assume the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt\ge 2\cdot10^{15}$, and either $\tau\le\sqrt3/2-2\beta$ (Region~I) or $\tau>1$. Then
\[
\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\ \le\ 7r+C_4,\qquad C_4:=1.3\cdot10^{26}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\emph{Status.} The argument was checked in two independent referee passes. Both found the conclusion correct and found no counterexample, but found gaps in the text of an earlier write-up: the three-sender case in the band $|t-\frac12|<\kappa$ was argued incorrectly (it is replaced by \cref{lem:Th} below), the packing step for thin $K$ did not follow as written (it is replaced by \cref{lem:Tstar}), several depths were misstated, and the error bookkeeping had not been written out (it was written out in the first referee pass, and is summarised in \cref{lem:flatmodel} and \cref{tab:margins}). All these repairs are incorporated here. Computer checks (\cref{app:verification}) are floating-point screenings and exact checks of special lattices; they are evidence, not part of the proof.

\emph{Proof of Theorem 1(b) for $\tau(X)\le\sqrt3/2-1/50$ and for $\tau(X)>1$, from \cref{thm:P4}.} In the degenerate regime the bound is $n+1\le\frac{21}{16}n+1$. In the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt<2\cdot10^{15}$ we have $\Delta<3.9\cdot10^{15}$, so $n\le(2\Delta+1)^2<6.1\cdot10^{31}$ and $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le3n<1.9\cdot10^{32}$. Otherwise the hypothesis $\tau(X)\le\sqrt3/2-1/50$ of Theorem~1(b) is Region~I ($2\beta=1/50$), where $e(S)\le s+C_{\mathrm{bad}}$ by \cref{sec:regionI}; for $\tau>1$ there are no rungs and $e(S)\le s+C_{\mathrm{bad}}$ by \cref{sec:common}. \Cref{lem:lpavg}(b) and \cref{thm:P4} give $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le\frac{21}{16}n+\frac38(C_{\mathrm{bad}}+\frac12C_4)<\frac{21}{16}n+2.5\cdot10^{25}$. So Theorem~1(b) holds in these ranges with $C_1=2\cdot10^{32}$; the remaining range $\sqrt3/2+3/100\le\tau(X)\le1$ is treated in \cref{sec:II}. \qed

Throughout this section $\tau\le\sqrt3/2-2\beta$ or $\tau>1$, and $\Dt\ge2\cdot10^{15}$. Since $t=\tau+(1-t^2)/(2\Dt)$, Region~I gives $t\le t_I:=\sqrt3/2-2\beta+10^{-15}<0.84604$, and $\tau>1$ gives $t>1$.

\subsection{Parameters, good points and the flat model}
We use \cref{lem:frame,lem:DN} with
\[
\eps_0=10^{-6},\quad\eps=\tan\eps_0,\quad\Lambda=10^9,\quad\rho=6,
\]
so that $\Lambda(1-\cos\eps_0)<10^{-3}$, and we put $\delta_0:=10^{-4}$ and $\kappa:=10^{-2}$. A point $y\in R\setminus D$ with $h\le1$ is \emph{good$'$} if the perimeter of $K$ is at least $2\Lambda$, $\turn(\gamma^\Lambda_y)\le\eps_0$ and $\partial K\cap\Dd(y,6)\subseteq\gamma^\Lambda_y$. (Since $\partial K$ consists of arcs of radius $\Dt$, good$'$ points exist only if $\Dt\ge2\Lambda/\eps_0=2\cdot10^{15}$.)

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:fewbad2}
Fewer than $N'_{\mathrm g}:=2.6\cdot10^{25}$ points $y\in R$ with $v(y)\ge7$ are not good$'$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
As in \cref{lem:fewbad}: if the perimeter of $K$ is less than $2\Lambda$ then $|X\setminus D|\le(2\Lambda+1)^2<4.1\cdot10^{18}$; the turning condition fails for fewer than $2\pi(2\Lambda+3)^2/\eps_0<2.52\cdot10^{25}$ points; and if some $b\in\partial K\cap\Dd(y,6)$ is not on $\gamma^\Lambda_y$, then $|p_0b|\le7$ and \cref{lem:Tstar} with $\ell=7$ gives $|X\setminus D|<10^5$.
\end{proof}

Let $y$ be good$'$. We use the frame of \cref{lem:frame} and call $q_z$ the \emph{level} of $q\in X\cap\Dd(y,6)$; for an interior point $x$ we call $-x_z$ its \emph{depth}.

\begin{lemma}[flat model]\label{lem:flatmodel}
Let $y$ be good$'$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item every point of $(S\setminus D)\cap\Dd(y,6)$ has level in $[-6\eps,0]\subseteq[-\delta_0,0]$, and every interior point has level $<f\le0$;
\item there is a number $H\ge t-\delta_0/2$ such that every point of $D\cap\Dd(y,6)$ has level in $[H,H+\delta_0/2]$;
\item \emph{(pinning)} if $k\in(S\setminus D)\cap\Dd(y,6)$ and $z\in D\cap\Dd(y,6)$, then $|(z_z-k_z)-t|\le1.3\cdot10^{-5}$; so if $\Dd(y,6)$ contains a point of $S\setminus D$ and a point of $D$, we may take $H\in[t-\delta_0/2,t+\delta_0/2]$;
\item if $y'$ is good$'$ and $|y-y'|\le3$, the levels of a point of $\Dd(y,6)\cap\Dd(y',6)$ in the two frames, measured from the respective boundary lines, differ by less than $2\cdot10^{-5}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) is (G1), (G2), (G4) with $|f(x)|\le6\eps$ for $|x|\le6$. (b) By (G3) every level is at least $t-6\eps$, and by \cref{lem:DN} with $\xi=\zeta=6$ we have $\kappa_{\mathrm D}<2\cdot10^{-6}$, so two levels differ by less than $12\kappa_{\mathrm D}<2.4\cdot10^{-5}$; let $H$ be the smallest level. (c) As in the proof of \cref{lem:pin}: put $\Delta c:=z_z-k_z\ge t-6\eps$. The inward normal $u_k$ is within $\eps_0$ of $-n_0$ by (G5), and $|z_x-k_x|\le12$. If $\Delta c>12\tan\eps_0$, then $(z-k)\cdot u_k<0$, so $z$ is an exception for $k$ and $(z-k)\cdot u_k=-t+(d^2-t^2)/(2\Dt)$ with $d\le12$; this gives $|\Delta c-t|\le12\eps_0+13\eps_0^2+80/\Dt<1.3\cdot10^{-5}$. Otherwise $t-6\eps\le\Delta c\le12\tan\eps_0$, and again $|\Delta c-t|\le12\eps<1.3\cdot10^{-5}$. With (a) and (b) all levels of $D\cap\Dd(y,6)$ are within $1.3\cdot10^{-5}+6\eps+2.4\cdot10^{-5}<\delta_0/2$ of $t$. (d) The frames differ by a rotation of at most $\eps_0$ (G5) and the boundary lines by at most $6\eps$ near these points.
\end{proof}

So, up to errors of at most $\delta_0$ in levels, the neighbourhood of a good$'$ point looks like a \emph{flat model}: K-row points at level $0$, diametral points at a common level $H\ge t$ (with $H=t$ as soon as a K-row point is within distance $6$), interior points below level $0$. Directions of unit vectors are determined from levels up to $\delta_0/|\sin\theta|$, i.e.\ to within $0.007^\circ$ for directions at least $60^\circ$ from the vertical and to within $0.9^\circ$ near the vertical. Every inequality used below holds with a margin larger than these errors; the smallest margins are listed in \cref{tab:margins}.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
margin & value & used in\\
\midrule
fans excluded: $t>0.8659$ versus $t\le t_I<0.8461$ & $0.0198$ & \cref{lem:S3}\\
type Dt receivers: $m\le1$ (depth $\ge0.8845$ versus $\frac{\sqrt3}2$) & $0.0185$ & \cref{lem:recv2}\\
$C_\varphi$ receivers in band L: depth $>1$ & $0.019$ & \cref{lem:recv2}\\
sender directions in bands L and H versus $\pm60^\circ$ & $0.65^\circ$ & \cref{lem:loads}\\
band Th: depths forced by a $0^\circ$ and by a $\pm60^\circ$ sender & $0.42$ & \cref{lem:Th}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The smallest margins in \cref{sec:P4}, against level errors below $5\cdot10^{-5}$ and angle errors below $0.01^\circ$ (away from the vertical).}\label{tab:margins}
\end{table}

For a neighbour $q$ of $y$ we use $\psi$ and the height $c(q)=\cos\psi=q_z+h$ as in \cref{sec:N3}. By \cref{lem:flatmodel}: a K-neighbour has $c\in[h-\delta_0,h]$ and $|\psi|<90^\circ+\eps_0$; a D-neighbour has $c\in[h+H-\delta_0,h+H+\delta_0]$; an interior neighbour has $c<h$. In particular \emph{a neighbour with $c>h$ is a D-neighbour}. The proof of \cref{lem:pairs} now gives: two K-neighbours, or two D-neighbours, of $y$ form a mirror pair up to $|\psi_1+\psi_2|<0.012^\circ$, and there are at most two of each kind. For an interior point $x\in\Dd(y,5)$ at depth $d$ and a neighbour $q$ of $x$ in the direction $\theta$ from the upward vertical: if $q\in S\setminus D$ then $\cos\theta\in[d-\delta_0,d]$; if $q\in D$ then $\cos\theta\ge d+H-\delta_0$. In particular, \emph{an interior point of depth greater than $1+\delta_0$ has no neighbour in $S$}.

\subsection{Neighbours in \texorpdfstring{$S$}{S} and classification}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:S3}
Let $y$ be good$'$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item If $t>1+2\delta_0$, then $y$ has no D-neighbour.
\item $m_y\le3$.
\item If $m_y=3$, the neighbours of $y$ in $S$ are two K-neighbours at $\pm\psi_K$ with $60^\circ\le\psi_K<90^\circ+\eps_0$ and one D-neighbour at $\psi_D$ with $|\psi_D|\le\psi_K-60^\circ$ (up to $0.012^\circ$), and $\sin(\psi_K-30^\circ)-\delta_0\le t\le1-\cos\psi_K+\delta_0$. In particular $t\ge\frac12-\delta_0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) A D-neighbour would have $c\ge h+H-\delta_0\ge t-2\delta_0>1$. (b) If $m_y=4$, then as in the proof of \cref{lem:heavy} the neighbours in $S$ are K-neighbours within $\eps_0$ of $\pm90^\circ$ and D-neighbours within $\eps_0$ of $\pm30^\circ$. By \cref{lem:flatmodel}(c), $t$ is within $\delta_0$ of their height difference, so $t\ge\cos(30^\circ+\eps_0)-\sin\eps_0-\delta_0>0.8659$. This contradicts $t\le t_I<0.8461$ in Region~I, and (a) when $t>1$ (the D-neighbours have $c<0.87<1$). (c) By the pair property, three neighbours in $S$ are of kinds $\{K,K,D\}$ or $\{K,D,D\}$. In the second case the D-pair is at $\pm\psi_D$ with $\psi_D\ge30^\circ$, and the K-neighbour, at least $60^\circ$ from both and with $|\psi|<90^\circ+\eps_0$, forces $\psi_D\le30^\circ+\eps_0+0.012^\circ$ and a K-neighbour near $\pm90^\circ$; then $t>0.8659$ as in (b), impossible. In the first case the K-pair is at $\pm\psi_K$, and the D-neighbour, which has $c>0$, lies between them at least $60^\circ$ from both: $|\psi_D|\le\psi_K-60^\circ$. By \cref{lem:flatmodel}(c), $t=\cos\psi_D-\cos\psi_K$ up to $\delta_0$; now $\cos\psi_D\ge\cos(\psi_K-60^\circ)$ and $\cos(\psi_K-60^\circ)-\cos\psi_K=\sin(\psi_K-30^\circ)$, and $\cos\psi_D\le1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[classification]\label{lem:classP4}
Let $y$ be good$'$ with $v(y)\ge8$. Then, up to the reflection $\psi\mapsto-\psi$ and the errors of the flat model, $y$ is of one of the following types.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=4.2em]
\item[\textup{(A)}] $(6,2)$: two K-neighbours at $\pm30^\circ$; $h=\frac{\sqrt3}2$; the other four neighbours are interior. (If $t\le2\delta_0$, one of the two may be a D-neighbour in $S$.)
\item[\textup{(B)}] $(6,2)$: two D-neighbours in $S$ at $\pm30^\circ$; $h=\frac{\sqrt3}2-H>0$, so $H\in[t-\delta_0,\frac{\sqrt3}2)$; the neighbours at $\pm90^\circ$, $\pm150^\circ$ are interior.
\item[\textup{(C$_\varphi$)}] $(6,2)$, $0^\circ\le\varphi\le30^\circ$: a D-neighbour in $S$ at $\varphi$ and a K-neighbour at $\varphi-60^\circ$; $h=\cos(60^\circ-\varphi)$ and $t=\sin(30^\circ-\varphi)$; the neighbours at $\varphi+60^\circ$, $\varphi\pm120^\circ$, $\varphi+180^\circ$ are not in $S$, and those at $\varphi+120^\circ$, $\varphi+180^\circ$ are interior.
\item[\textup{(A$'$)}] $(6,2)$: K-neighbours at $\pm60^\circ$, a D-neighbour in $R$ at $0^\circ$; $h=\frac12$, $t=\frac12$; interior neighbours at $\pm120^\circ$, $180^\circ$.
\item[\textup{(cap)}] $(6,3)$: K-neighbours at $\pm60^\circ$, a D-neighbour in $S$ at $0^\circ$; $h=\frac12$, $t=\frac12$; interior neighbours at $\pm120^\circ$, $180^\circ$; $v(y)=9$.
\item[\textup{(Dt)}] $(5,3)$: as in \cref{lem:S3}(c), with $h=\cos\psi_K$ and two interior neighbours in the arc $I_y:=[\psi_K+60^\circ,300^\circ-\psi_K]$. In Region~I, $60^\circ\le\psi_K\le87.8^\circ$; for $\tau>1$ this type needs $t\le1+2\delta_0$ and $\psi_K>89.99^\circ$.
\end{enumerate}
Every type except the cap has $v(y)=8$. For $t>1+2\delta_0$ only type A occurs.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:S3}, $v\ge8$ leaves $(\deg,m)\in\{(6,2),(5,3),(6,3)\}$. For $\deg y=6$ the neighbours form a regular hexagon.

\emph{$(5,3)$ and $(6,3)$.} Use \cref{lem:S3}(c). If $\deg y=6$, the K-pair occupies mirror slots of the hexagon, so $\psi_K\in\{30^\circ,60^\circ,90^\circ\}$. For $30^\circ$ there is no room for the D-neighbour; for $90^\circ$ the D-neighbour is at $\pm30^\circ$ and $t>0.8659$, excluded as in \cref{lem:S3}; for $60^\circ$ the D-neighbour is at $0^\circ$ and $t=1-\frac12=\frac12$: the cap. If $\deg y=5$, the two other neighbours are not in $S$. They are not D-neighbours: a second D-neighbour would be the mirror image $-\psi_D$ of the first, at least $60^\circ$ away, forcing $\psi_D\approx\pm30^\circ$, $\psi_K\approx90^\circ$ and $t>0.8659$. So they are interior, and being at least $60^\circ$ from the K-neighbours and the D-neighbour, they lie in $I_y$. The range of $\psi_K$ follows from \cref{lem:S3}(c): $\sin(\psi_K-30^\circ)\le t_I+\delta_0$ gives $\psi_K\le87.8^\circ$, and $t>1$ gives $\cos\psi_K<\delta_0$.

\emph{$(6,2)$, two K-neighbours.} They are a mirror pair at $\pm30^\circ$, $\pm60^\circ$ or $\pm90^\circ$. At $\pm30^\circ$ this is type A ($h=\cos30^\circ$; the neighbours at $\pm90^\circ,\pm150^\circ$ have $c<h$ and so are interior). At $\pm60^\circ$ the neighbour at $0^\circ$ has $c=1>h=\frac12$, so it is a D-neighbour with $H=\frac12$, and $t=\frac12$ by pinning; since $m_y=2$ it lies in $R$: type A$'$. At $\pm90^\circ$ the neighbours at $\pm30^\circ$ have $c>h$, so they are D-neighbours and $t>0.8659$; excluded.

\emph{$(6,2)$, two D-neighbours.} They are a mirror pair, at $\pm30^\circ$, $\pm60^\circ$ or $\pm90^\circ$. At $\pm60^\circ$ the neighbour at $0^\circ$ has $c=1>h$, so it is a D-neighbour at a level about $\frac12$ higher than the other two, contradicting \cref{lem:flatmodel}(b). At $\pm90^\circ$ we get $h\le\delta_0$, and the neighbours at $\pm30^\circ$ are D-neighbours at a level about $\frac{\sqrt3}2$ higher; again a contradiction. At $\pm30^\circ$: $c=\frac{\sqrt3}2=h+H$, and $h>0$ because $y\in\operatorname{int}K$; the neighbours at $\pm90^\circ$ are not in $S$ (as $m_y=2$) and are not D-neighbours ($c<0.01<h+H-\delta_0$), so they are interior. This is type B. (Note $H<\frac{\sqrt3}2$ strictly: $H=\frac{\sqrt3}2$ would put $y$ on $\partial K$, but $R\setminus D\subseteq\operatorname{int}K$ by \cref{sec:structR}(b).)

\emph{$(6,2)$, a K- and a D-neighbour.} If $t\le2\delta_0$, both have $c$ within $4\delta_0$ of $h$, so they form a mirror pair, and as above they are at $\pm30^\circ$ (type A with one D-neighbour; at $\pm60^\circ$ and $\pm90^\circ$ a further D-neighbour at a different level would appear). So let $t>2\delta_0$; then every D-neighbour has $c>h$. Let $\varphi\in[-30^\circ,30^\circ]$ be the slot with the largest $c$; by symmetry $\varphi\ge0$. If the D-neighbour in $S$ were not at $\varphi$, the neighbour at $\varphi$, whose $c$ is at least that of the D-neighbour and hence greater than $h$, would be a second D-neighbour at the same level, hence its mirror image; then $\varphi=30^\circ$, the K-neighbour is at $-90^\circ$ and $t>0.8659$; excluded. So the D-neighbour is at $\varphi$. The slot $\varphi-60^\circ$ has the second largest $c$; if the K-neighbour were not there, that slot would have $c>h$ and be a second D-neighbour, which again forces $\varphi=30^\circ$ and is excluded (for $\varphi=0$ the slots $\pm60^\circ$ tie, and we name the K-neighbour's slot $\varphi-60^\circ$). So the K-neighbour is at $\varphi-60^\circ$, $h=\cos(60^\circ-\varphi)$, and by pinning $t=\cos\varphi-\cos(60^\circ-\varphi)=\sin(30^\circ-\varphi)$. The slots $\varphi+120^\circ$ and $\varphi+180^\circ$ have $c<0<h$ and are interior. This is type C$_\varphi$; for $\varphi=30^\circ$ it is type A with one D-neighbour.

Finally, for $t>1+2\delta_0$ there are no D-neighbours (\cref{lem:S3}(a)), which leaves type A.
\end{proof}

So in Region~I and for $\tau>1$ \emph{every good$'$ point has weight at most $8$, except caps, which have weight $9$ and need $t=\frac12$}. We now move the excess over $7$.

\subsection{Discharging}
Every good$'$ point $y$ with $v(y)\ge8$ sends charge to some of its interior neighbours, its \emph{receivers}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item types A and B: $\frac12$ to each of the neighbours at $\pm150^\circ$;
\item type C$_\varphi$: $\frac12$ to each of the neighbours at $\varphi+120^\circ$ and $\varphi+180^\circ$;
\item type A$'$: $1$ to the neighbour at $180^\circ$;
\item cap: $1$ to the neighbour at $180^\circ$ and $\frac12$ to each of the neighbours at $\pm120^\circ$;
\item type Dt: $\frac12$ to each of its two interior neighbours.
\end{itemize}
Then every receiver $x$ with $v(x)=7$ (a \emph{forwarder}) passes all the charge it has received to its neighbour opposite its unique neighbour in $S$ (\cref{lem:recv2}(c) shows that this is well defined). Depths and directions at a receiver are measured in the frame of the good$'$ point from which the charge originates; by \cref{lem:flatmodel}(d) these frames agree up to the flat-model errors.

\begin{lemma}[receivers]\label{lem:recv2}
Let $x$ be a receiver of a good$'$ point $y$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item The depth of $x$ is: $\sqrt3$ for type A; $h+\frac{\sqrt3}2\ge\frac{\sqrt3}2$ for type B; $2\cos(60^\circ-\varphi)\ge1$ at $\varphi+120^\circ$ and $\sqrt3\cos(30^\circ-\varphi)\ge\frac32$ at $\varphi+180^\circ$ for type C$_\varphi$; $\frac32$ at $180^\circ$ for A$'$ and caps; $1$ at $\pm120^\circ$ for caps; at least $\sin(\psi_K+30^\circ)$ for type Dt.
\item $m_x\le1$, so $v(x)\le7$ and $x$ is not a sender. Moreover $m_x=0$ unless $y$ is of type Dt, or a cap and $x$ is at $\pm120^\circ$.
\item If $v(x)=7$, then $\deg x=6$, the unique neighbour of $x$ in $S$ is a K-row point in the direction $\beta$ with $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$ from the vertical (with $|\beta|\le28^\circ$ in Region~I), and the neighbour $g$ of $x$ in the direction $\beta+180^\circ$ has depth greater than $1.7$, so $m_g=0$ and $v(g)\le6$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) The depth of the neighbour of $y$ at $\psi$ is $h-\cos\psi$. For type C$_\varphi$ use $\cos(60^\circ-\varphi)+\cos\varphi=\sqrt3\cos(30^\circ-\varphi)$; for type Dt use $\cos\psi_K-\cos(\psi_K+60^\circ)=\sin(\psi_K+30^\circ)$ and $\psi_x\in I_y$.

(b) Depth greater than $1+\delta_0$ gives $m_x=0$; this covers type A, C$_\varphi$ at $\varphi+180^\circ$, and A$'$ and caps at $180^\circ$. \emph{Type B:} as seen from $x$ (at $\pm150^\circ$ from $y$), $y$ is in the direction $\mp30^\circ$ and the interior neighbour $w$ of $y$ at $\pm90^\circ$ is in the direction $\pm30^\circ$ (since $e(90^\circ)-e(150^\circ)=e(30^\circ)$ for $e(\theta):=(\sin\theta,\cos\theta)$). A neighbour of $x$ in $S$ would be in a direction $\theta$ with $\cos\theta\ge\frac{\sqrt3}2-\delta_0$, i.e.\ $|\theta|\le30.01^\circ$, and at least $60^\circ$ from both $\pm30^\circ$; impossible. \emph{Type C$_\varphi$ at $\varphi+120^\circ$:} a neighbour in $S$ would be a K-row point (a D-point needs $\cos\theta>1$) with $\cos\theta\ge2\cos(60^\circ-\varphi)-\delta_0$; this forces $\varphi<0.01^\circ$ and $|\theta|<0.9^\circ$. But the neighbour $w$ of $y$ at $\varphi+60^\circ$ is in the direction $\varphi$ from $x$ (as $e(\varphi+60^\circ)-e(\varphi+120^\circ)=e(\varphi)$), so that K-row point would be within $0.02$ of $w$, hence equal to $w$; and $w\notin S$. So $m_x=0$. \emph{Caps at $\pm120^\circ$:} the depth is $1$ up to $2\delta_0$, a D-point would need $\cos\theta>1$, and a K-row neighbour needs $|\theta|\le1.5^\circ$; two such are less than $60^\circ$ apart, so $m_x\le1$. \emph{Type Dt:} a D-neighbour of $x$ would need $\cos\theta\ge\sin(\psi_K+30^\circ)+H-\delta_0$. In Region~I, $\psi_K\le87.8^\circ$ gives depth at least $\sin117.8^\circ>0.8845$, and $H\ge t-\delta_0\ge\frac12-2\delta_0$, so this is impossible; K-row neighbours need $\cos\theta\ge0.8845$, i.e.\ $|\theta|<28^\circ$, and two of them would be less than $60^\circ$ apart. For $\tau>1$ we have $\psi_K>89.99^\circ$ and depth at least $\sin119.99^\circ>0.8659$; again there is no D-neighbour, and K-row neighbours have $|\theta|\le30.02^\circ$. Two of them would have to be within $0.04^\circ$ of $\pm30^\circ$, which forces the depth of $x$ to be within $10^{-3}$ of $\frac{\sqrt3}2$ and hence $\psi_x$ within $0.2^\circ$ of $150^\circ$ or $210^\circ$; then $y$ is within $0.2^\circ$ of the direction $\mp30^\circ$ from $x$, i.e.\ within $0.01$ of one of these K-row points, which is impossible since $y\in R$.

(c) If $v(x)=7$ then $m_x=1$ and $\deg x=6$, so by (b) $x$ is a receiver of type Dt or a cap receiver at $\pm120^\circ$, and its neighbour in $S$ is a K-row point in the direction $\beta$ found in (b). The six neighbours of $x$ form a regular hexagon, so $g$ exists, and its depth is the depth $d$ of $x$ plus $\cos\beta\ge d-\delta_0$, so at least $2d-\delta_0\ge\min\{2\cdot0.8845,\ 2\cdot0.8659,\ 2-3\delta_0\}-\delta_0>1.7$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[cone]\label{lem:cone}
Let $x$ receive charge from $s$, either directly or by forwarding. The direction $\theta$ from $x$ to $s$ and the depth of $s$ are as follows: types A and B: $\theta=\pm30^\circ$, depth $\frac{\sqrt3}2$ (A) or at most $\frac{\sqrt3}2-t+\delta_0$ (B); type C$_\varphi$: $\theta=\varphi-60^\circ$ (from its receiver at $\varphi+120^\circ$) or $\theta=\varphi$ (from its receiver at $\varphi+180^\circ$), depth $\cos(60^\circ-\varphi)$; A$'$ and caps: $\theta=0^\circ$ (one unit), and for caps also $\theta=\mp60^\circ$ (half a unit), depth $\frac12$; type Dt: $\theta\in[\psi_K-120^\circ,120^\circ-\psi_K]$, depth $\cos\psi_K$; a forwarder: $\theta=\beta$ with $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$. In each case the depth of $x$ is the depth of $s$ plus $\cos\theta$. All these directions lie in $[-60^\circ,60^\circ]$; so $x$ has at most three senders, and three only in the directions $-60^\circ,0^\circ,60^\circ$ up to $1^\circ$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $x$ is the neighbour of $s$ at $\psi$, then $s$ is the neighbour of $x$ in the direction $\psi-180^\circ$. The list follows from \cref{lem:classP4,lem:recv2}. Senders of $x$ are distinct neighbours of $x$, hence at least $60^\circ$ apart.
\end{proof}

We split Region~I and the range $\tau>1$ into four bands, which together cover them:
\[
\text{L: } t\le\tfrac12-\kappa;\qquad \text{Th: } |t-\tfrac12|<\kappa;\qquad \text{H: } \tfrac12+\kappa\le t\le t_I\ \text{ or }\ 1<t\le1+2\delta_0;\qquad \text{T: } t>1+2\delta_0 .
\]

\begin{lemma}[band Th]\label{lem:Th}
Let $|t-\frac12|<\kappa$ and let $x$ receive charge. A sender of $x$ in a direction within $3^\circ$ of $\pm60^\circ$ forces the depth of $x$ to be at most $1.06$; a sender of $x$ in a direction within $3^\circ$ of $0^\circ$ forces the depth of $x$ to be at least $1.48$. Consequently $x$ never has both kinds of sender.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In this band the heavy types are A, B, C$_\varphi$ with $\varphi\le0.67^\circ$ (from $\sin(30^\circ-\varphi)\ge\frac12-\kappa-\delta_0$), A$'$, caps, and Dt with $60^\circ\le\psi_K\le60.67^\circ$ (from $\sin(\psi_K-30^\circ)\le\frac12+\kappa+\delta_0$). Types A and B send only from $\pm30^\circ$. A sender within $3^\circ$ of $\pm60^\circ$ is a C$_\varphi$ (direction $\varphi-60^\circ$), a cap (direction $\mp60^\circ$) or a Dt with $|\theta|\ge57^\circ$; its depth is at most $\cos59.33^\circ+\delta_0<0.511$, so the depth of $x$ is at most $0.511+\cos57^\circ<1.06$. A sender within $3^\circ$ of $0^\circ$ is a C$_\varphi$ (direction $\varphi$; depth of $x$ equal to $\sqrt3\cos(30^\circ-\varphi)\ge1.49$), an A$'$ or a cap (depth of $x$ equal to $\frac32$), a Dt with $|\theta|\le3^\circ$ (depth of $x$ at least $\cos60.67^\circ+\cos3^\circ>1.48$), or a forwarder; a forwarder in this band is a receiver of a Dt or a cap, so its depth is at least $\min\{\sin90.67^\circ,1-2\delta_0\}>0.999$, its K-row neighbour is in a direction $\beta$ with $\cos\beta>0.998$, i.e.\ $|\beta|<3^\circ$, and the depth of $x$ is at least $0.999+\cos3^\circ>1.99$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[loads]\label{lem:loads}
Every point of $R$ receives at most one unit of charge in total.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $x$ receive charge. \emph{Band L.} The heavy types are A, B and C$_\varphi$, and $t=\sin(30^\circ-\varphi)\le\frac12-\kappa+\delta_0$ gives $\varphi\ge0.65^\circ$; types A$'$, cap and Dt need $t\ge\frac12-\delta_0$. By \cref{lem:recv2}(b) all receivers have $m=0$, so there is no forwarding. The sender directions $\pm30^\circ$, $\pm(60^\circ-\varphi)$, $\pm\varphi$ lie in $[-59.35^\circ,59.35^\circ]$, an interval shorter than $120^\circ$, so $x$ has at most two senders, each giving $\frac12$.

\emph{Band H.} The heavy types are A, B and Dt, and $1-\cos\psi_K+\delta_0\ge t\ge\frac12+\kappa$ gives $\psi_K\ge60.65^\circ$, so Dt sends from directions in $[-59.35^\circ,59.35^\circ]$. By \cref{lem:recv2}, the forwarders are receivers of type Dt, and all their senders are of type Dt (receivers of A and B have $m=0$). A forwarder $x'$ has only one sender: two Dt senders at $\delta_1<\delta_2$ in $[-59.35^\circ,59.35^\circ]$ with $\delta_2-\delta_1\ge60^\circ$ have $\delta_1<-0.65^\circ$ and $\delta_2>0.65^\circ$, and the K-row neighbour of $x'$, at $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$ and at least $60^\circ$ from both, would need $\delta_1+60^\circ\le\beta\le\delta_2-60^\circ$, i.e.\ $\delta_2-\delta_1\ge120^\circ$. So every forwarder passes on exactly $\frac12$, from a direction $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$. Hence all senders of $x$ give at most $\frac12$ and lie in $[-59.35^\circ,59.35^\circ]$, and there are at most two of them.

\emph{Band T.} Only type A occurs (\cref{lem:classP4}); its receivers have $m=0$, and the senders lie at $\pm30^\circ$: at most two, each giving $\frac12$.

\emph{Band Th.} By \cref{lem:cone}, three senders would lie near $-60^\circ$, $0^\circ$ and $60^\circ$, which \cref{lem:Th} forbids; so $x$ has at most two senders. A sender giving more than $\frac12$ is an A$'$ or a cap in the direction $0^\circ$, or a forwarder passing on $1$, in a direction within $3^\circ$ of $0^\circ$. Any other sender would be at least $57^\circ$ from the vertical, hence (being within $60^\circ$ of it) within $3^\circ$ of $\pm60^\circ$, which \cref{lem:Th} forbids. So either $x$ has a single sender, giving at most $1$, or at most two senders, each giving $\frac12$. (A forwarder receives only half units, from at most two senders, so it passes on $\frac12$ or $1$; forwarders are not themselves forwarding targets, since targets have $m=0$.)
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:P4}]
Give each $y\in R$ the charge $v(y)$ and apply the rules; the total is unchanged. A good$'$ point of weight $8$ sends $1$ and a cap sends $2$; neither receives anything (receivers have $v\le7$), so both end at $7$. A forwarder has weight $7$ and passes on all it receives, so it ends at $7$. Every other receiver has weight at most $6$ (\cref{lem:recv2}) and receives at most $1$ (\cref{lem:loads}), so it ends at most at $7$. Every other good$'$ point has weight at most $7$, and so do the points of $R\cap D$ and those with $h>1$ (weight at most $6$). What remains are the points that are not good$'$ and have weight at least $8$: there are fewer than $N'_{\mathrm g}$ of them (\cref{lem:fewbad2}); they are not receivers, and they end at most at $12$. Hence $\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\le7r+5N'_{\mathrm g}<7r+1.3\cdot10^{26}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:P4}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em,label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{Radius-one discharging stops at $7$.} In an exact stack of hexagonal rows below a straight K-row and in the exact ``cap lattice'' at $t=\frac12$ (coordinates in $\mathbb Q(\sqrt3)$, script \texttt{r6\_below\_exact\_examples.py}), the average weight on $R$ is exactly $6$ at every truncation depth, but the weights per period are $8,6,\dots,6,4$ and $9,7,6,\dots,6,5,3$ respectively. Any rule that moves charge only between neighbours leaves some point at $7$ in these examples. So $\frac{21}{16}$ is the limit of rules of the kind used here.
\item \emph{What would give $\frac97$ in Region~I.} By \cref{rem:lpvalues}, an average weight $6+o(1)$ on $R$ in Region~I would give $\frac97$ there, matching the lower bound. No configuration with average weight above $6$ was found in a search over clusters of up to $7$ points of $R$ (\cref{app:verification}); we state this as \cref{q:avg6}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Region II: Theorem 1(b) for \texorpdfstring{$\sqrt3/2+3\beta\le\tau\le1$}{sqrt3/2 + 3 beta <= tau <= 1}}\label{sec:II}

In Region~II the bound $e(S)\le s+p+C$ of \cref{sec:regionII} carries the full $p$-term, and by \cref{rem:lpvalues} this caps the method at $\frac43$ whatever the average weight on $R$. In this section we show that in Region~II with $\tau\le1$ the $p$-term can be halved, $e(S)\le s+\frac12p+C$ (\cref{thm:IIe}), and that the average weight on $R$ is at most $7$ there as well (\cref{thm:IIw}). Halving the $p$-term is exactly what the weights $\frac58,\frac38$ of \cref{lem:lpavg}(b) can absorb (\cref{lem:IIlp}). Together with \cref{sec:P4} this gives Theorem~1(b) on both sides of Region~III.

\begin{theorem}[Theorem 1(b) in full]\label{thm:IIone}
Let $X\subset\R^2$ be a set of $n$ points. If $\tau(X)\le\sqrt3/2-1/50$ or $\tau(X)\ge\sqrt3/2+3/100$, then
\[
\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\ \le\ \tfrac{21}{16}\,n+C_1,\qquad C_1=2\cdot10^{32}.
\]
\end{theorem}
So the only range of $\tau$ in which the bound is still $\frac43n+C_0$ is Region~III, $\sqrt3/2-1/50<\tau(X)<\sqrt3/2+3/100$.

\emph{Status.} The two new inputs, \cref{thm:IIe} and \cref{thm:IIw}, have each been through one independent referee pass. Both passes found the results correct and found no counterexample; they asked for a missing step in the disjointness of the arcs below (\cref{lem:IIdisj}, the case of two components with the same centre), a corrected justification of the count of shared endpoints (proof of \cref{thm:IIe}), a justification of the crossing argument (\cref{lem:IIord}), explicit constants (\cref{lem:IIcons}), a direct proof of $m_y\le3$ that does not go through fans (\cref{lem:IIS}), a shorter proof that receivers have $m\le1$ (\cref{lem:IIrecv}), and a correct statement of how the frames of different senders are compared (\cref{lem:IIloads}). These corrections are incorporated below. \Cref{thm:IIe,thm:IIw} are not formalised. Computer checks (\cref{sec:IIchecks}) are exact checks of constants and of special configurations, and floating-point screenings; they are evidence, not part of the proof.

\begin{lemma}[linear combination with half the $p$-term]\label{lem:IIlp}
If $e(S)\le s+\frac12p+C$ and $\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\le7r+C''$, then $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le\frac{21}{16}n+\frac38\bigl(C+\frac12C''\bigr)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:identity}, $\mult(\delta)\le s+\frac12p+\frac72r+C+\frac12C''$. By \cref{lem:VSQ}, $\mult(\Dt)\le\frac32s-\frac12p$; here $Q$ is, by definition, the set of \emph{all} points of $S$ of $\Dt$-degree exactly $1$, which is what the proof of \cref{lem:VSQ} uses, and $p=|Q|$ is the same quantity as in the bound on $e(S)$. Hence
\[
\tfrac58\bigl(\tfrac32s-\tfrac12p\bigr)+\tfrac38\bigl(s+\tfrac12p+\tfrac72r+C+\tfrac12C''\bigr)=\tfrac{21}{16}(s+r)-\tfrac18p+\tfrac38\bigl(C+\tfrac12C''\bigr),
\]
and $-\frac18p\le0$.\chk{LP}
\end{proof}
(In the terms of \cref{rem:lpvalues}: with $e(S)\le s+\frac12p+O(1)$ the weight on $\mult(\Dt)$ must be at least $\frac12$ to cancel the $p$-term, and the value is $\max\{\frac54,\frac{3c}{2c+1}\}$ for average weight $2c$. For $2c=8,7,6$ this is $\frac43,\frac{21}{16},\frac97$.\chk{LP})

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:IIone} from \cref{thm:IIe,thm:IIw}]
If $n\le3$ or $X$ has only one distance there is nothing to prove. In the degenerate regime $\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le n+1\le\frac{21}{16}n+1$ (\cref{prop:degenerate}). In the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt<2\cdot10^{15}$ the bound $3n<1.9\cdot10^{32}$ of the proof of Theorem~1(b) in \cref{sec:P4} applies. So let $\Dt\ge2\cdot10^{15}$. If $\tau\le\sqrt3/2-1/50$ or $\tau>1$, Theorem~1(b) is proved in \cref{sec:P4}. Otherwise $\sqrt3/2+3\beta\le\tau\le1$ (recall $3\beta=3/100$), and \cref{thm:IIe}, \cref{thm:IIw} and \cref{lem:IIlp} give
\[
\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le\tfrac{21}{16}n+\tfrac38\bigl(C_{\mathrm{bad}}+87+\tfrac12C_4\bigr)<\tfrac{21}{16}n+2.5\cdot10^{25}.
\]
So $C_1=2\cdot10^{32}$ still suffices.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Rungs in Region II: \texorpdfstring{$e(S)\le s+\frac12p+C$}{e(S) <= s + p/2 + C}}\label{sec:IIe}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:IIe}
In the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt\ge10^4$ and $\sqrt3/2+3\beta\le\tau\le1$,
\[
e(S)\ \le\ s+\tfrac12p+C_{\mathrm{bad}}+87 .
\]
\end{theorem}

Throughout this subsection we assume the hypotheses of \cref{thm:IIe}. We use the notation of \cref{sec:common,sec:regionII}: $\theta_0=\arccos\tau<26.4^\circ$, $\sigma=\sqrt{1-\tau^2}\le\sigma_{\max}<0.4441$,\chk{C5} $t\le1$ and $t\ge\tau$. All rungs below are good rungs; $\rho$ is their number, and by (R1) of \cref{sec:regionII} every vertex is an end of at most one of them. We put
\[
\alpha_1:=2\arcsin\frac1{2\Delta},\qquad \alpha_2:=2\arcsin\frac1{2\Dt},\qquad \varphi_\tau:=\arcsin\frac{\sigma}{\Delta}\in[0,\pi/2).
\]
So two points of $\Cc(w,\Delta)$ (resp.\ $\Cc(w,\Dt)$) at distance $1$ are at angle $\alpha_1$ (resp.\ $\alpha_2$) about $w$, and $\alpha_2>\alpha_1$. For a point $w$ we write $\vartheta(x)$ for the polar angle of $x$ about $w$; it will only be used on arcs of angular width less than $\pi$, on which we fix a continuous branch. ``Counterclockwise'' refers to this angle. Recall from \cref{sec:regionIII} (the paragraph on the rotational sense and \cref{lem:cyclic}) that in both rows ``right'' means counterclockwise along the boundary, and that a right T-step between two points of $\Cc(w,\Delta)$ is counterclockwise about $w$; the same holds, with the same proof, for a right T-step between two points of $\Cc(w,\Dt)\cap\partial K$.

\begin{lemma}[rung geometry]\label{lem:IIrung}
Let $(v,z)$ be a rung with $v\in S\setminus D$, and let $w:=w_v$, $u:=u_v$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $z-w=-(\Dt+\tau)u\pm\sigma u^\perp$ and $|z-w|=\Delta$. Hence $\vartheta(z)=\vartheta(v)+\varsigma\varphi_\tau$ for a sign $\varsigma=\varsigma_{(v,z)}\in\{\pm1\}$, the \emph{sign} of the rung \textup{(}if $\sigma=0$ we put $\varsigma:=+1$\textup{)}.
\item $2\varphi_\tau<\alpha_1$.
\item Along the segment from $v$ to $z$ the distance to $w$ increases strictly from $\Dt$ to $\Delta$, and $\vartheta$ is monotone. So the segment is the graph $\{\vartheta=g(\varrho)\}$ of a continuous monotone function $g$ of $\varrho=|x-w|\in[\Dt,\Delta]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) By \cref{lem:identities}(c), $z-v=-\tau u\pm\sigma u^\perp$, and $v-w=-\Dt u$. So $|z-w|^2=(\Dt+\tau)^2+\sigma^2=\Dt^2+2\Dt\tau+1=\Delta^2$, by the definition $\tau=(\Delta^2-\Dt^2-1)/(2\Dt)$ (\cref{sec:setup}). The component of $z-w$ along $(v-w)/\Dt=-u$ is $\Dt+\tau>0$ and the orthogonal component has length $\sigma$, so the angle at $w$ between $v$ and $z$ is $\arcsin(\sigma/\Delta)=\varphi_\tau$.\chk{IIh}
(b) $\sin\varphi_\tau=\sigma/\Delta<1/(2\Delta)=\sin(\alpha_1/2)$, since $\sigma<\frac12$ in Region~II. (In fact $2\varphi_\tau/\alpha_1\le2\sigma<0.8881$.)\chk{IIh}
(c) For $x=v+\lambda(z-v)$, $\lambda\in[0,1]$, we have $x-w=-(\Dt+\lambda\tau)u\pm\lambda\sigma u^\perp$, so $|x-w|^2=(\Dt+\lambda\tau)^2+\lambda^2\sigma^2$ is strictly increasing in $\lambda$ (as $\tau>0$), and the angle between $x-w$ and $-u$ is $\arctan(\lambda\sigma/(\Dt+\lambda\tau))$, which is nondecreasing in $\lambda$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[a rung-carrying D-row component lies on one circle]\label{lem:IIZ}
Let $(v,z)$ be a rung, $w:=w_v$, and let $Z$ be the component of $z$ in the graph of pure D-row T-edges.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $Z\subseteq\Cc(w,\Delta)$; consecutive points of $Z$ are at angle exactly $\alpha_1$ about $w$, all in the counterclockwise sense; $Z$ spans an angle at most $\pi/3$ about $w$; and $Z$ is a path.
\item If $|Z|\ge2$ and $(v',z')$ is any rung with $z'\in Z$, then $w_{v'}=w$.
\end{enumerate}
We call $w$ the \emph{centre} of $Z$; by \textup{(b)} it does not depend on the rung when $|Z|\ge2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) By \cref{lem:IIrung}(a), $|zw|=\Delta$, so $w$ is a diametral partner of $z$ and $\hat w:=(w-z)/\Delta$ is an inward normal of $P$ at $z$ (\cref{lem:identities}(a) and the remark after it). The angle between $\hat w$ and $u_v=(w-v)/\Dt$ is the angle at $w$ of the triangle $vzw$; it is opposite the side $vz$ of length $1$, hence acute, and by the law of sines it is at most $\arcsin(1/\Delta)$. Also $\angle(u_v,u_z)\le\beta$ because the rung is good. Let $z_1$ be a T-neighbour of $z$ in $Z$ (on either side). Then $(z_1-z)\cdot u_z\in(0,\beta)$, so $\angle(z_1-z,u_z)\ge\pi/2-\arcsin\beta$ and
\[
\eta:=(z_1-z)\cdot\hat w\le\sin\bigl(\arcsin\beta+\beta+\arcsin(1/\Delta)\bigr)<0.0201<t .\chk{IIh}
\]
By \cref{lem:B} (whose threshold is at least $t$ since $t\le1$), $|z_1w|=\Delta$. From here on the proof of \cref{lem:Bprime} applies verbatim: a vertex $z_k\in Z\cap\Cc(w,\Delta)$ that has both a left and a right T-neighbour has a normal cone within $\arcsin\beta$ of $u_{z_k}$, and its other T-neighbour is again on $\Cc(w,\Delta)$; inducting outwards from $z$ (every vertex reached after the first step has the vertex it was reached from as a T-neighbour, so it is an interior vertex whenever it has a further T-neighbour) puts every vertex of $Z$ on $\Cc(w,\Delta)$. The induction never starts at an end vertex of $Z$ other than $z$ itself, and at $z$ the direction $\hat w$ is controlled by the rung triangle as above. The statements about angles, the counterclockwise sense, the span and the path structure follow as in \cref{lem:Bprime}.

(b) As $|Z|\ge2$ and $Z$ is connected, $z'$ has a T-neighbour $z_1'\in Z$. By (a) applied to $(v',z')$, $z',z_1'\in\Cc(w_{v'},\Delta)$, and by (a) applied to $(v,z)$, $z',z_1'\in\Cc(w,\Delta)$. If $w\neq w_{v'}$, these are two circles of radius $\Delta$ whose centres are points of $X$, at distance $d\le\Delta$; they meet in two points at distance $2\sqrt{\Delta^2-d^2/4}\ge\sqrt3\,\Delta>1$, which contradicts $|z'z_1'|=1$. So $w_{v'}=w$.
\end{proof}

By (R1) at D-vertices, a component $Z$ with $|Z|=1$ carries at most one rung. For a component $Z$ let $m_Z$ be the number of rungs whose D-end lies in $Z$; so $\sum_Zm_Z=\rho$, and $m_Z\ge2$ implies $|Z|\ge2$. For such $Z$, with centre $w$, all these rungs have K-ends on $\Cc(w,\Dt)$ (\cref{lem:IIZ}(b)), and by \cref{lem:IIrung}(a) all their endpoints lie in an arc of angular width at most $\pi/3+2\varphi_\tau<\pi$ about $w$.

\begin{lemma}[order]\label{lem:IIord}
Let $Z$ have centre $w$ and let $(v_A,z_A)\neq(v_B,z_B)$ be rungs with $z_A,z_B\in Z$ and $\vartheta(z_A)<\vartheta(z_B)$. Then $\vartheta(v_A)<\vartheta(v_B)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By (R1) the four points are distinct, and $v_A,v_B\in\Cc(w,\Dt)$, so $\vartheta(v_A)\neq\vartheta(v_B)$. Suppose $\vartheta(v_A)>\vartheta(v_B)$. By \cref{lem:IIrung}(c), each rung is the graph of a continuous function $g_A$, $g_B$ of $\varrho\in[\Dt,\Delta]$, inside an annular sector of opening less than $\pi$. Now $g_A-g_B$ is positive at $\varrho=\Dt$ and negative at $\varrho=\Delta$, so it vanishes at some $\varrho^*\in(\Dt,\Delta)$: the two segments cross at a point $x$ that is interior to both. If the four points are not collinear, the triangle inequality gives $|v_Av_B|+|z_Az_B|<|v_Ax|+|xv_B|+|z_Ax|+|xz_B|=|v_Az_A|+|v_Bz_B|=2$, so one of $|v_Av_B|,|z_Az_B|$ is less than $1$, a contradiction. If they are collinear, both segments lie on one line through $x$, along which $|\cdot-w|$ increases from the $v$-end to the $z$-end on both segments (\cref{lem:IIrung}(c)); at the interior point $x$ this is the same direction of the line, so both $v_A$ and $v_B$ are the point of that line at distance $\Dt$ from $w$ on the same side of $x$, i.e.\ $v_A=v_B$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection*{Gaps}
Let $Z$ be a component with $m_Z\ge2$ and centre $w$. Order its rungs $A_1,\dots,A_{m_Z}$ by $\vartheta$ of their D-ends (distinct by (R1)). A \emph{gap} is a pair $(A,B)=(A_i,A_{i+1})$ of consecutive rungs; $Z$ has $m_Z-1$ gaps. Write $A=(v_A,z_A)$, $B=(v_B,z_B)$ and $\varsigma_A,\varsigma_B$ for their signs. By \cref{lem:IIZ}(a), $\vartheta(z_B)-\vartheta(z_A)=k\alpha_1$ with an integer $1\le k$, $k\alpha_1\le\pi/3$; by \cref{lem:IIrung}(a) and \cref{lem:IIord},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IIgapangle}
0<\vartheta(v_B)-\vartheta(v_A)=k\alpha_1-(\varsigma_B-\varsigma_A)\varphi_\tau<\pi .
\end{equation}
Let $\gamma_{AB}$ be the arc of $\Cc(w,\Dt)$ from $v_A$ counterclockwise to $v_B$.

\begin{lemma}[the arc of a gap]\label{lem:IIarc}
Let $(A,B)$ be a gap.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $\gamma_{AB}\subseteq\partial K$.
\item Follow pure right T-edges in the K-row from $v_0:=v_A$: $v_0,v_1,\dots$. As long as $v_i\in\gamma_{AB}\setminus\{v_B\}$ and $v_i$ has a pure right T-neighbour, that neighbour is the point of $\gamma_{AB}$ at angle $\alpha_2$ counterclockwise from $v_i$. Consequently either the walk reaches $v_B$, which happens only if $\vartheta(v_B)-\vartheta(v_A)=j\alpha_2$ with an integer $j\ge1$, and then $v_1,\dots,v_{j-1}$ are K-row points in the relative interior of $\gamma_{AB}$; or it stops at a K-row point $b\in\gamma_{AB}\setminus\{v_B\}$ that has no pure right T-neighbour.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) This is the paragraph ``The arc between consecutive apexes'' in \cref{sec:regionIII}, which uses only that $v_A,v_B\in K\cap\Cc(w,\Dt)$ are less than $\pi$ apart about $w$ \eqref{eq:IIgapangle}. (b) The proof of \cref{lem:rigid} uses only (a), the fact that $w_{v_A}=w$ (here \cref{lem:IIZ}(b)), and $v_B\in X$; it applies verbatim. The walk cannot pass $v_B$, since a point of $\gamma_{AB}$ at angle in $(0,\alpha_2)$ from $v_B$ is at distance less than $1$ from it.
\end{proof}

We call the gap \emph{unbroken} if the walk of \cref{lem:IIarc}(b) reaches $v_B$, and \emph{broken} otherwise; $U$ denotes the number of unbroken gaps, and $U_Z$ the number in $Z$. For an unbroken gap, $j\ge1$ is its number of K-steps and $k\ge1$ its number of D-steps, and \eqref{eq:IIgapangle} reads
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IIstar}
j\alpha_2=k\alpha_1-(\varsigma_B-\varsigma_A)\varphi_\tau .
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[disjointness of gap arcs]\label{lem:IIdisj}
Two closed arcs $\gamma_{AB}\neq\gamma_{A'B'}$ of gaps are disjoint, except when the gaps are consecutive gaps $(A,B)$, $(B,B')$ of the same component, which share exactly the point $v_B$. In particular the half-open arcs $[v_A,v_B):=\gamma_{AB}\setminus\{v_B\}$ of all gaps are pairwise disjoint.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Same component $Z$.} By \cref{lem:IIord} the K-ends of the rungs of $Z$ are in the same order about $w$ as their D-ends, so the arcs of the gaps of $Z$ are consecutive intervals of angles, $[\vartheta(v_{A_i}),\vartheta(v_{A_{i+1}})]$; two of them meet only if they are consecutive, in the common endpoint.

\emph{Different components $Z\neq Z'$ with the same centre $w$.} Both lie on $\Cc(w,\Delta)$, and all points of $X$ on this circle lie in an arc of width at most $\pi/3$ (they are pairwise at most $\Delta$ apart).
