A point of $Z'$ at angle less than $\alpha_1$ from a point of $Z$ would be at distance less than $1$ from it, and it cannot coincide with it. Since consecutive points of $Z$ are exactly $\alpha_1$ apart, every angle in $[\min\vartheta(Z),\max\vartheta(Z)]$ is within $\alpha_1/2$ of a point of $Z$; so no point of $Z'$ has its angle in this range, and every point of $Z'$ is at angle at least $\alpha_1$ from $\min\vartheta(Z)$ and from $\max\vartheta(Z)$. As consecutive points of $Z'$ are also exactly $\alpha_1$ apart, $Z'$ cannot have points on both sides of the range of $Z$. Hence the angular ranges $[\min\vartheta(Z),\max\vartheta(Z)]$ and $[\min\vartheta(Z'),\max\vartheta(Z')]$ are disjoint and at least $\alpha_1$ apart. By \cref{lem:IIrung}(a) the K-ends of the rungs of $Z$ lie in $[\min\vartheta(Z)-\varphi_\tau,\max\vartheta(Z)+\varphi_\tau]$, and likewise for $Z'$. Since $2\varphi_\tau<\alpha_1$ (\cref{lem:IIrung}(b)), these two closed intervals are disjoint. The arcs of the gaps of $Z$ lie in the first, those of $Z'$ in the second.

\emph{Different centres $w\neq w'$.} Let $x$ be a common point of $\gamma_{AB}\subseteq\Cc(w,\Dt)$ and $\gamma_{A'B'}\subseteq\Cc(w',\Dt)$, both nondegenerate arcs of $\partial K$ (\cref{lem:IIarc}(a)). If $x$ were in the relative interior of $\gamma_{AB}$, then near $x$ the boundary $\partial K$ coincides with $\gamma_{AB}$ on both sides of $x$, and $\gamma_{A'B'}$ would contain a nondegenerate arc of $\partial K$ lying on both circles, forcing $w=w'$. So $x$ is an endpoint of both arcs, i.e.\ the K-end of a rung $A$ or $B$ and of a rung $A'$ or $B'$. By (R1), $x$ is the K-end of only one rung; the D-end of that rung lies in one component, and the centre of that component is $w_x$ (\cref{lem:IIZ}(b)). So both gaps have centre $w_x$, a contradiction.

For the last statement: the only common point of two closed gap arcs is $v_B$ for consecutive gaps $(A,B),(B,B')$, and $v_B\notin[v_A,v_B)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[break count]\label{thm:IIBC}
$e(S)\le s+U+70+C_{\mathrm{bad}}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If the K-row or the D-row has at most $2$ points, then $\rho\le2$ by (R1), and $e(S)\le e_T+\rho+C_{\mathrm{bad}}\le s+2+C_{\mathrm{bad}}$ by (T1). Otherwise define $B_K,B_D$ as in \cref{sec:regionIII}; by \cref{lem:cyclic} and \cref{lem:mixedT} (fewer than $71$ mixed T-edges in Region~II), as in~\eqref{eq:eTbreaks},
\[
e_T\le s-B_K-B_D+70,
\]
and a row point with no pure right T-neighbour is the left element of a pair counted in $B_K$ or $B_D$.

\emph{K-breaks.} By \cref{lem:IIarc}(b) every broken gap $(A,B)$ has a K-row point $b_{AB}\in[v_A,v_B)$ with no pure right T-neighbour. By \cref{lem:IIdisj} these points are distinct for distinct gaps, so they give distinct pairs in $B_K$, and $B_K\ge\sum_Z(m_Z-1-U_Z)$, the sum over components with $m_Z\ge1$.

\emph{D-breaks.} Every component $Z$ with $m_Z\ge1$ is a path (\cref{lem:IIZ}(a); this includes $|Z|=1$), and its counterclockwise last vertex has no pure right T-neighbour; distinct components give distinct such vertices. So $B_D\ge\#\{Z:m_Z\ge1\}$.

Adding, $B_K+B_D\ge\sum_Z(1+m_Z-1-U_Z)=\rho-U$, and
\[
e(S)\le e_T+\rho+C_{\mathrm{bad}}\le s-(\rho-U)+70+\rho+C_{\mathrm{bad}}=s+U+70+C_{\mathrm{bad}}.\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[signs of unbroken gaps]\label{lem:IIcons}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item At most $6$ unbroken gaps have $\varsigma_A=\varsigma_B$.
\item At most $24$ rungs $B$ are shared by two unbroken gaps $(A,B)$ and $(B,C)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Arithmetic.} Suppose $J\alpha_2=K\alpha_1$ with integers $J\ge1$, $K\ge1$. Since $\alpha_2>\alpha_1$, $K>J$, so $J(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)=(K-J)\alpha_1\ge\alpha_1$ and $K\alpha_1\ge1/(1/\alpha_1-1/\alpha_2)$. As in the paragraph ``The path cannot land on $v_B$'' of \cref{sec:regionIII}, $1/\alpha_1-1/\alpha_2\le t+1/(23\Dt)$, and $t\le1$, $\Dt\ge10^4$ give $J\alpha_2=K\alpha_1>0.99999$.\chk{IIh}

(a) If $\varsigma_A=\varsigma_B$, \eqref{eq:IIstar} reads $j\alpha_2=k\alpha_1$, so $\gamma_{AB}$ has angle greater than $0.99999$ and length greater than $0.99999\,\Dt$. By \cref{lem:IIdisj} the half-open arcs are disjoint, and the perimeter of $K\subseteq\Dd(w,\Dt)$ is at most $2\pi\Dt$; as $2\pi/0.99999<6.3$, there are at most $6$ such gaps.\chk{IIh}

(b) Let $(A,B)$ and $(B,C)$ be unbroken gaps of one component. If one of them has equal signs, $B$ is one of the at most $2\cdot6$ rungs of the gaps in (a). Otherwise $\varsigma_A=-\varsigma_B=\varsigma_C$, and adding~\eqref{eq:IIstar} for the two gaps gives $(j+j')\alpha_2=(k+k')\alpha_1$; the walk from $v_A$ reaches $v_C$ along $\gamma_{AB}\cup\gamma_{BC}$, and by the arithmetic above this arc has length greater than $0.99999\,\Dt$. Each point of $\partial K$ lies in at most one half-open gap arc, hence in at most two of the half-open arcs $[v_A,v_C)$, so there are at most $4\pi/0.99999<12.6$ such rungs $B$. In total at most $12+12=24$.\chk{IIh}
\end{proof}
(By the same arithmetic, same-sign unbroken gaps are impossible when $t<3/\pi-1/(23\Dt)$, since then $k\alpha_1>\pi/3$; we do not need this.)

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:IIe}]
We show $2U\le p+34$; with \cref{thm:IIBC} this gives $e(S)\le s+\frac12p+17+70+C_{\mathrm{bad}}$.

\emph{Endpoints.} Let $(A,B)$ be a gap. Its K-ends $v_A,v_B$ are rung K-ends. A rung K-end of $\Dt$-degree at least $2$ is of type (i) or of type (ii) (\cref{lem:corner} and \cref{sec:regionII}). There are at most $5$ vertices of type (i), each the K-end of at most one rung, which is an end rung of at most two gaps; so at most $10$ pairs (gap, endpoint) involve a vertex of type~(i). If $v$ is of type (ii), its rung partner $z$ has no T-neighbour (\cref{lem:C}), so its component is $\{z\}$, $m_{\{z\}}=1$, and the rung is in no gap. All other gap endpoints have $\Dt$-degree $1$, i.e.\ lie in $Q$.

\emph{Interior walk points.} Let $(A,B)$ be unbroken, with walk $v_A=v_0,v_1,\dots,v_j=v_B$. For $0<i<j$, $v_i$ is a K-row point in the relative interior of $\gamma_{AB}\subseteq\Cc(w,\Dt)\cap\partial K$, and $|v_iw|=\Dt$. Suppose $|v_iw'|=\Dt$ for some $w'\in X\setminus\{w\}$. Since $K\subseteq\Dd(w',\Dt)$, $\gamma_{AB}$ is contained in $\Cc(w,\Dt)\cap\Dd(w',\Dt)$, which is a closed arc whose endpoints are the points of $\Cc(w,\Dt)\cap\Cc(w',\Dt)$. So $v_i$ is an endpoint of that arc, and cannot be a relative interior point of $\gamma_{AB}$. Hence $v_i\in Q$.

So the closed arc $\gamma_{AB}$ of an unbroken gap contains the $j+1\ge2$ distinct K-row points $v_0,\dots,v_j$, all in $Q$ except for the type-(i) endpoints counted above.

\emph{Counting.} By \cref{lem:IIdisj} two closed arcs of distinct gaps share a point only if they are consecutive gaps $(A,B),(B,C)$, and then they share only $v_B$. (A shared endpoint $v_B=v_{A'}$ means, by (R1), $B=A'$; equal left ends, or equal right ends, give the same gap.) By \cref{lem:IIcons}(b) this happens for at most $24$ rungs $B$ with both gaps unbroken. Hence
\[
2U-10\le\sum_{(A,B)\text{ unbroken}}\#\bigl(Q\cap\gamma_{AB}\bigr)\le p+24,
\]
and $2U\le p+34$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:IIsharp}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em,label=(\alph*)]
\item The factor $\frac12$ cannot be improved by an argument of this local kind. Take $w=0$, K-row points on $\Cc(0,\Dt)$ and D-row points on $\Cc(0,\Delta)$, with rungs of alternating signs and one K-step and one D-step per gap, at the value of $\tau$ (with $1-\tau=1/(8\Dt^2)+O(\Dt^{-4})$) at which~\eqref{eq:IIstar} holds with $j=k=1$. In $60$-digit arithmetic at $\Dt=10^4$ (script \texttt{r7\_A2\_configs.py}, configuration A) all distances in a window of $8$ gaps are at least $1$, the unit pairs are exactly the expected T-edges and rungs, and the window has $e-s=U-1$ and $2U$ points of $\Dt$-degree $1$. This is a local configuration; whether it extends to a set with $\Theta(n)$ unbroken gaps is not known.
\item Conversely, a short argument (the radial ``shadow'' of a K-row point contains no point of the D-row) suggests that $U=O(1)$ in Region~II except on a single curve $\alpha_2-\alpha_1=2\varphi_\tau$; this has not been refereed and is not used here.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Average weight at most 7 in Region II}\label{sec:IIw}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:IIw}
Assume the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt\ge2\cdot10^{15}$ and $\sqrt3/2+3\beta\le\tau\le1$. Then
\[
\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\ \le\ 7r+C_4,\qquad C_4=1.3\cdot10^{26}.
\]
\end{theorem}

Throughout this subsection we assume the hypotheses of \cref{thm:IIw}. Then $\tau\le1$ gives $t\le1$ and $t\ge\tau\ge t_{\mathrm{II}}:=\sqrt3/2+3\beta>0.89602$ (\cref{sec:setup}).\chk{IIh}

\emph{What is used from \cref{sec:N3,sec:P4}.} We use the parameters $\eps_0=10^{-6}$, $\Lambda=10^9$, $\rho=6$, $\delta_0=10^{-4}$ and the notion of a good$'$ point of \cref{sec:P4}. The following statements are proved in \cref{sec:N3,sec:P4} without using any hypothesis on $\tau$ (the standing assumption of \cref{sec:P4} that $\tau\le\sqrt3/2-2\beta$ or $\tau>1$ is not used in their proofs), and we use them verbatim: \cref{sec:structR}, \cref{lem:frame} (in particular (G5)), \cref{lem:DN}, \cref{lem:fewbad2}, \cref{lem:flatmodel}, and the consequences stated after \cref{tab:margins}: for a good$'$ point $y$ and a neighbour $q$ of $y$ at angle $\psi$ with height $c(q)=\cos\psi$, a K-neighbour has $c\in[h-\delta_0,h]$ and $|\psi|<90^\circ+\eps_0$, a D-neighbour (in $S$ or in $R$) has $c\in[h+H-\delta_0,h+H+\delta_0]$ with $H\ge t-\delta_0/2$, an interior neighbour has $c<h$, and so \emph{a neighbour with $c>h$ is a D-neighbour}; two K-neighbours, or two D-neighbours, form a mirror pair up to $0.012^\circ$, and there are at most two of each kind; and for an interior point $x\in\Dd(y,5)$ at depth $d$ and a neighbour $q$ of $x$ in direction $\theta$ from the upward vertical, $\cos\theta\in[d-\delta_0,d]$ if $q\in S\setminus D$ and $\cos\theta\ge d+H-\delta_0$ if $q\in D$; in particular an interior point of depth greater than $1+\delta_0$ has no neighbour in $S$. By \cref{sec:structR}(b) every neighbour of $y$ is a K-neighbour, a D-neighbour or an interior neighbour, and the neighbours in $S$ are the K-neighbours and the D-neighbours in $S$; there are no points of $R$ on $\partial K$, and $h>0$. The Region-dependent results \cref{lem:S3,lem:classP4,lem:recv2,lem:cone,lem:Th,lem:loads} are \emph{not} used; they are replaced by \cref{lem:IIS,lem:IIclass,lem:IIrecv,lem:IIloads}. We do not use pinning (\cref{lem:flatmodel}(c)) either: only the lower bound $H\ge t-\delta_0/2$ on the level of diametral points enters.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:IIS}
Let $y$ be good$'$. Then $y$ has at most one D-neighbour, and every D-neighbour has $|\psi|<26.38^\circ$. Consequently $m_y\le3$, and if $m_y=3$ the neighbours of $y$ in $S$ are two K-neighbours and one D-neighbour.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A D-neighbour has $c\ge h+H-\delta_0\ge t-\frac32\delta_0\ge t_{\mathrm{II}}-\frac32\delta_0>0.89587>\cos26.38^\circ$, since $h>0$.\chk{IIh} Two neighbours are at least $60^\circ$ apart as seen from $y$, so they cannot both lie in the open cone $|\psi|<26.38^\circ$. There are at most two K-neighbours, and every neighbour in $S$ is a K-neighbour or a D-neighbour.
\end{proof}
This replaces the fan analysis of \cref{sec:N3,sec:P4}: in particular there are no fans (F6, F5, HF) and no caps among good$'$ points in Region~II.

\begin{lemma}[classification]\label{lem:IIclass}
Let $y$ be good$'$ with $v(y)\ge8$. Then $v(y)=8$ and $y$ is of one of the following types.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=4.6em]
\item[\textup{(A)}] $(\deg y,m_y)=(6,2)$: the neighbours form a regular hexagon within $0.012^\circ$ of $\{\pm30^\circ,\pm90^\circ,\pm150^\circ\}$; the two near $\pm30^\circ$ are K-neighbours and the other four are interior; $h\ge\sqrt3/2$; the neighbours near $\pm150^\circ$ have depth greater than $1.73$.
\item[\textup{(Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$)}] $(\deg y,m_y)=(5,3)$: K-neighbours at $\psi_-<0<\psi_+$ with $|\psi_\pm|\in[84.02^\circ,90.006^\circ]$, one D-neighbour in $S$ at $\psi_D$ with $|\psi_D|<26.38^\circ$, and two interior neighbours with angles in the arc $I_y:=[\psi_++60^\circ,\ \psi_-+300^\circ]$; moreover $h\le1-t+\frac32\delta_0<0.10413$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:IIS}, $m_y\le3$, and $\deg y\le6$; so $v(y)\ge8$ leaves $(\deg y,m_y)\in\{(6,2),(6,3),(5,3)\}$.

\emph{$\deg y=6$.} Six neighbours pairwise at least $60^\circ$ apart form a regular hexagon $\{\varphi+60^\circ k\}$ with $\varphi\in[-30^\circ,30^\circ]$. The slot at $\varphi$ has $c\ge\cos30^\circ$, and one of the slots $\varphi\pm60^\circ$ has $|\psi|\le60^\circ$, hence $c\ge\frac12$. If $h<\frac12$, both of these slots have $c>h$ and are D-neighbours, contradicting \cref{lem:IIS}. So $h\ge\frac12$; then a D-neighbour would need $c\ge h+H-\delta_0\ge\frac12+t_{\mathrm{II}}-\frac32\delta_0>1.39$, which is impossible, so $y$ has no D-neighbour and every slot has $c\le h$. The slot at $\varphi$ gives $h\ge\sqrt3/2$. A K-neighbour then has $c\ge h-\delta_0\ge\sqrt3/2-\delta_0$, i.e.\ $|\psi|\le30.012^\circ$.\chk{IIh} Two slots of the hexagon are $60^\circ$ apart, so at most two slots qualify, and two only if they lie within $0.012^\circ$ of $\pm30^\circ$. Hence $m_y\le2$, $(6,3)$ is impossible, and $(6,2)$ is type A: the other four neighbours are not in $S$ and are not D-neighbours, so they are interior (\cref{sec:structR}(b)). The neighbours near $\pm150^\circ$ have depth $h-\cos\psi\ge\sqrt3/2+\cos30.012^\circ>1.7319$.\chk{IIh}

\emph{$(\deg y,m_y)=(5,3)$.} By \cref{lem:IIS} the neighbours in $S$ are two K-neighbours, a mirror pair at $\psi_-<0<\psi_+$, and a D-neighbour in $S$ at $|\psi_D|<26.38^\circ$. Since $c(\text{D-neighbour})\le1$, we get $h\le1-H+\delta_0\le1-t+\frac32\delta_0\le1-t_{\mathrm{II}}+\frac32\delta_0<0.10413$. Each K-neighbour has $\cos\psi_\pm\in[h-\delta_0,h]\subseteq[-\delta_0,0.10413]$, so $|\psi_\pm|\in[84.02^\circ,90.006^\circ]$.\chk{IIh} The two remaining neighbours are not in $S$, and are not D-neighbours by \cref{lem:IIS}; so they are interior. Each is at least $60^\circ$ from $\psi_-$, $\psi_D$ and $\psi_+$. It cannot lie in the upper arc $(\psi_-,\psi_+)$: there it would lie between $\psi_D$ and $\psi_+$ (or $\psi_-$) at least $60^\circ$ from both, which needs $\psi_+-\psi_D\ge120^\circ$, i.e.\ $\psi_+>93.6^\circ$. So both lie in $I_y$.
\end{proof}

So in Region~II every good$'$ point has weight at most $8$. We move the excess over $7$.

\subsubsection*{Discharging}
Every good$'$ point $y$ with $v(y)=8$ sends charge to two of its interior neighbours, its \emph{receivers}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item type A: $\frac12$ to each of the neighbours near $\pm150^\circ$;
\item type Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$: $\frac12$ to each of its two interior neighbours.
\end{itemize}
Then every receiver $x$ with $v(x)=7$ (a \emph{forwarder}) passes all the charge it has received to the neighbour $g$ of $x$ with $g-x=x-k$, where $k$ is the unique neighbour of $x$ in $S$ (\cref{lem:IIrecv}(c) shows that $k$ is unique and $g$ exists). The point $g$ is the \emph{target} of $x$.

\begin{lemma}[receivers]\label{lem:IIrecv}
Let $x$ be a receiver of a good$'$ point $y$; depths and directions at $x$ are measured in the frame of $y$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item If $y$ is of type A, then $x$ has depth greater than $1.73$, so $m_x=0$ and $v(x)\le6$.
\item If $y$ is of type Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$, then $x$ has depth $d_x\in[0.86597,1.10413]$; $x$ has no D-neighbour; every K-neighbour of $x$ is in a direction $\beta$ with $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$; $y$ is seen from $x$ in a direction $\theta_y$ with $|\theta_y|\le35.98^\circ$; and $m_x\le1$, so $v(x)\le7$.
\item If $v(x)=7$, then $y$ is of type Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$, $\deg x=6$, $x$ has exactly one neighbour $k$ in $S$, a K-row point in a direction $\beta$ with $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$, and the target $g=2x-k$ is a neighbour of $x$ of depth greater than $1.73$; so $m_g=0$ and $v(g)\le6$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular every receiver and every target lies in $R\setminus D$ and has $m\le1$, so it is not a sender.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Receivers are interior neighbours of $y$, so they lie in $\operatorname{int}K$ and hence in $R\setminus D$. The depth of the neighbour of $y$ at angle $\psi$ is $h-\cos\psi$.

(a) By \cref{lem:IIclass}. An interior point of depth greater than $1+\delta_0$ has no neighbour in $S$.

(b) Let $x$ be at angle $\psi_x\in I_y$. If $\psi_x\le180^\circ$, then $\cos\psi_x\le\cos(\psi_++60^\circ)$ and $h\ge\cos\psi_+$, so $d_x\ge\cos\psi_+-\cos(\psi_++60^\circ)=\sin(\psi_++30^\circ)\ge\sin120.006^\circ>0.86597$, as $\sin$ is decreasing on $[114^\circ,120.006^\circ]$; the case $\psi_x\ge180^\circ$ is symmetric with $\psi_-$.\chk{IIh} Also $d_x\le h+1<1.10413$. A D-neighbour of $x$ would need $\cos\theta\ge d_x+H-\delta_0>0.86597+0.8958>1$; so there is none. A K-neighbour has $\cos\beta\ge d_x-\delta_0>0.86587$, i.e.\ $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$.\chk{IIh} As seen from $x$, $y$ is in the direction $\theta_y=\psi_x-180^\circ\in[\psi_+-120^\circ,\psi_-+120^\circ]\subseteq[-35.98^\circ,35.98^\circ]$.\chk{IIh}

Suppose $x$ had two K-neighbours, at $\beta_1<\beta_2$. They are at least $60^\circ$ apart and $|\beta_i|\le30.02^\circ$, so $\beta_1\le-29.98^\circ$ and $\beta_2\ge29.98^\circ$. The point $y\in R$ is a third neighbour of $x$, at least $60^\circ$ from both. If $\theta_y\le\beta_1$, then $\beta_1-\theta_y\le-29.98^\circ+35.98^\circ<60^\circ$; if $\theta_y\ge\beta_2$, then likewise $\theta_y-\beta_2<60^\circ$. So $\beta_1<\theta_y<\beta_2$, and then $\beta_2-\beta_1\ge120^\circ>60.04^\circ$. This contradiction gives $m_x\le1$. (This argument does not depend on $t$; in particular it covers the case $|\psi_\pm|\approx90^\circ$, $h\approx0$, which occurs for every $t\in[t_{\mathrm{II}},1]$.)

(c) By (a), $y$ is of type Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$, and by (b) $m_x=1$ and $\deg x=6$, with $k$ a K-row point at $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$. Six neighbours pairwise at least $60^\circ$ apart form a regular hexagon, so $g=x-(k-x)$ is a neighbour of $x$. Its depth is $d_x+\cos\beta\ge2d_x-\delta_0>1.7318$.\chk{IIh} As $g\in\Dd(y,2)$, it has no neighbour in $S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[loads]\label{lem:IIloads}
A forwarder receives exactly $\frac12$. Every point of $R$ receives at most $1$ in total, directly or by forwarding.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Frames.} Let $x$ receive charge. Its \emph{senders} are good$'$ points of type A or Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$ of which $x$ is a receiver, and forwarders of which $x$ is the target; a forwarder need not be good$'$, and all statements about it are made in the frame of its own (good$'$, type Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$) sender. Every direct sender of $x$ is at distance $1$ from $x$, and the sender of a forwarder of $x$ is at distance at most $2$ from $x$. So all good$'$ points involved lie within distance $4\le\rho-1$ of each other, and by (G5) their frames differ by rotations of at most $\eps_0$. Directions of unit vectors between points of $X$ therefore agree in all these frames up to $\eps_0<10^{-4}$ degrees, which is negligible against the margins below (at least $24^\circ$). The quantities $m$ and $v$ do not depend on a frame.

\emph{Forwarders.} A forwarder has $v=7$, hence $m=1$; by \cref{lem:IIrecv}(a),(c) it is not a receiver of a type-A point and not a target. So all its senders are of type Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$, at directions in $[-35.98^\circ,35.98^\circ]$, and its neighbour $k\in S$ is at $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$. Suppose it had two senders, at $\delta_1<\delta_2$. Then $\delta_2-\delta_1\ge60^\circ$ gives $\delta_1\le-24.02^\circ$ and $\delta_2\ge24.02^\circ$. The neighbour $k$ is at least $60^\circ$ from both: if $\beta$ lies between them this needs $\delta_2-\delta_1\ge120^\circ>71.96^\circ$, and otherwise $|\beta|\ge84^\circ$. Both are impossible. So a forwarder has exactly one sender and passes on exactly $\frac12$.

\emph{Loads.} As seen from $x$, a type-A sender is within $0.012^\circ$ of $\pm30^\circ$ (its receivers are near $\pm150^\circ$), a type-Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$ sender is in $[-35.98^\circ,35.98^\circ]$ (\cref{lem:IIrecv}(b)), and a forwarder $x'$ with target $x$ is in the direction of its neighbour $k$ as seen from $x'$, i.e.\ at $|\beta|\le30.02^\circ$ (\cref{lem:IIrecv}(c)). Senders of $x$ are distinct neighbours of $x$, pairwise at least $60^\circ$ apart, in a window of width less than $72^\circ<120^\circ$; so $x$ has at most two senders. Each gives $\frac12$. Hence the load of $x$ is at most $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:IIw}]
Give each $y\in R$ the charge $v(y)$ and apply the rules; charge moves only between points of $R$, so the total is unchanged. A good$'$ point of weight $8$ (type A or Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$) sends $1$ and receives nothing (receivers and targets have $m\le1$, hence weight at most $7$), so it ends at $7$. A forwarder has weight $7$, receives $\frac12$ and passes it on, so it ends at $7$. Every other receiver or target has weight at most $6$ (\cref{lem:IIrecv}) and receives at most $1$ (\cref{lem:IIloads}), so it ends at most at $7$. Points of $R\cap D$ are never receivers (receivers are interior points of $K$). Every other point keeps its weight; this is at most $7$ unless the point has weight at least $8$ and is not good$'$ (\cref{lem:IIclass}). By \cref{lem:fewbad2} there are fewer than $N'_{\mathrm g}=2.6\cdot10^{25}$ such points; they never receive, and they end at most at $12$ (\cref{lem:identity}). Hence $\sum_{y\in R}v(y)\le7r+5N'_{\mathrm g}<7r+1.3\cdot10^{26}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:IIw}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em,label=(\alph*)]
\item The fan windows of \cref{lem:pin} ($t\in[0.75,0.95]$) come from the coarse parameter $\eps_0=1/50$ of \cref{sec:N3}. With the parameters of \cref{sec:P4}, \cref{lem:IIS} shows directly that in Region~II ($t\ge0.896$) no good$'$ point has two D-neighbours, so fans and caps do not occur there.
\item As in \cref{rem:P4}(a), the exact stack of hexagonal rows below a straight K-row has average weight $6$ on $R$ but per-period weights $8,6,\dots,6,4$, independently of $t$; so radius-one discharging stops at $7$ in Region~II as well. An average weight $6+o(1)$ in Region~II would give $\frac97$ there by \cref{lem:IIlp} and \cref{rem:lpvalues}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Computations for this section}\label{sec:IIchecks}
The checks marked \chk{IIh} (and \chk{LP} in \cref{lem:IIlp}) are elementary numerical inequalities; they were recomputed with $40$-digit arithmetic. The following scripts (in \texttt{scripts/} of the repository) were run with the repository's Python environment; none of them is part of the proof.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item \texttt{r7\_A2\_constants.py}: the identity $|z-w|=\Delta$ of \cref{lem:IIrung}(a) (symbolically), the bound $2\varphi_\tau/\alpha_1<0.8881$, the counts $6$ and $24$ of \cref{lem:IIcons}, and the values of the linear combination. All checks pass.
\item \texttt{r7\_A2\_configs.py}: exact ($60$-digit) configurations in the single-centre model near $\tau=1$ and near $\tau=\sqrt3/2+3\beta$, including configuration A of \cref{rem:IIsharp}(a), and two candidate configurations that turn out not to be realisable. No configuration violates \cref{thm:IIBC}, \cref{lem:IIcons} or \cref{thm:IIe}.
\item \texttt{r7\_A2\_shadow.py}: the computations behind \cref{rem:IIsharp}(b) (not used).
\item \texttt{r7\_B\_margins.py}: $60$-digit interval checks of the angular and depth margins of \cref{sec:IIw} (with slightly more conservative constants than in the text). All pass.
\item \texttt{r7\_B\_lattice\_t1.py}: an exact rational model at $t=1$; in $2\cdot10^4$ random dense configurations the heavy points are of types A and Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$ only, and the final charge is at most $7$.
\item \texttt{r7\_B\_classify.py} and \texttt{r7\_B\_rules.py}: a slot-model scan over $t\in[0.896,1]$ (only types A and Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$ occur, maximal weight $8$), and a floating-point beam search over $1.8\cdot10^5$ configurations of the discharging (maximal final charge $7$). They import the helper files \texttt{r6\_below\_classify\_B1copy.py} and \texttt{r6\_below\_rule\_check\_B1copy.py}.
\item \texttt{r7\_refB\_margins.py}, \texttt{r7\_refB\_classify.py}, \texttt{r7\_refB\_search.py}, \texttt{r7\_refB\_exact.py}: the referee's independent implementation for \cref{sec:IIw}: $60$-digit margins (all pass, including the linear combination), a classification scan (only types A and Dt$_{\mathrm{II}}$), a floating-point search and an exact search ($9506$ configurations, maximal final charge $7$).
\end{itemize}


\section{The regime \texorpdfstring{$\tau>1$}{tau > 1}: proof of Theorem 1(c)}\label{sec:Eprime}

When $\tau>1$ the diametral endpoints are far from $K$, and the $\delta$-graph splits into a part on $D$ and a part inside $K$. We bound the part inside $K$ by a global argument (\cref{thm:Eprime}) that uses no local classification at all. In this section $G$ is the $\delta$-graph of $X$ (scaled so that $\delta=1$), and for a subgraph $H$ we write
\[
\operatorname{Def}(H):=3|V(H)|-|E(H)| .
\]
Since $G$ has maximum degree at most $6$, $\operatorname{Def}(H)\ge0$ for every induced subgraph, and $\mult(\delta)=|E(G)|=3n-\operatorname{Def}(G)$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:Eprime}
Let $X$ be a finite planar set with minimum distance $1$, $n=|X|\ge1$ and $\Delta\le1.94\,\Dt$, and let $K=\bigcap_{w\in X}\Dd(w,\Dt)$. Let $Y\subseteq X\cap K$ and $A:=Y\cap\partial K$. Then
\[
\operatorname{Def}(G[Y])\ \ge\ 2|A|-C_E\,n^{2/3},\qquad C_E:=3\cdot10^4 .
\]
In particular, in the nondegenerate regime, $\operatorname{Def}(G[X\setminus D])\ge2|S\setminus D|-3\cdot10^4\,n^{2/3}$, for every $\tau$.
\end{theorem}
For the last statement take $Y=X\setminus D$: by \cref{lem:ballpoly}(a) and \cref{sec:structR}(b), $X\setminus D\subseteq K$ and $(X\setminus D)\cap\partial K=S\setminus D$. The constants depend only on $\Delta\le1.94\,\Dt$, not on $t$.

\emph{Status.} \Cref{thm:Eprime} and \cref{cor:tau1} were checked in three independent referee passes. The first two found the argument correct but found constant-level gaps in an earlier write-up (the window around a corner in Step~6 omitted nearby corners; the laminarity claim of Step~6 was stated for all gap arcs; the case of small $n$ was missing; a factor in Step~3; the winding argument of \cref{lem:winding} was not written out). The complete corrected proof below incorporates these repairs, and the third pass, on this corrected proof, found no errors or gaps. The numerical inequalities are checked in exact arithmetic by \texttt{r6\_Eprime\_full\_checks.py} (34 assertions).

\subsection{Classical facts}
We use the following standard facts.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(P\arabic*)},leftmargin=3em]
\item \emph{$G$ is a plane straight-line graph.} Its edges are unit segments between points at mutual distance at least $1$. A point of $X$ in the relative interior of an edge $ab$ would be within $\frac12$ of $a$ or $b$; so no vertex lies inside an edge, and no two edges overlap collinearly or touch at an interior point. If two edges $ab$ and $cd$ crossed properly, the diagonals of the convex quadrilateral $acbd$ would give $|ac|+|bd|<|ab|+|cd|=2$, which is impossible.
\item \emph{Faces and boundary walks.} For a plane graph with $\mathrm{cc}\ge1$ components, $V-E+F=1+\mathrm{cc}$, and $\sum_f|f|=2E$, where $|f|$ is the total length of the boundary walks of the face $f$ (see~\cite{MoharThomassen}). Hence
\[
\operatorname{Def}=3+3\,\mathrm{cc}+\sum_f(|f|-3).
\]
A bounded face of a simple plane graph has a cycle in its boundary, so $|f|\ge3$. The outer face has $|f_{\mathrm{out}}|=\sum_j|w_j|$, summed over the components $C_j$ incident with it, where $w_j$ is the outer boundary walk of $C_j$.
\item \emph{Separation and perimeter.} If a compact set $\Gamma\subset\R^2$ separates a convex body $Q$ from $\infty$ and $\Gamma\cap Q=\emptyset$, then $\mathcal H^1(\Gamma)\ge\operatorname{per}Q$. Indeed the nearest-point map $P_Q$ is $1$-Lipschitz~\cite{Schneider} and maps $\Gamma$ onto $\partial Q$: for $b\in\partial Q$ with outer normal $\nu$, the ray $b+\R_+\nu$ meets $\Gamma$, and $P_Q=b$ on that ray.
\item Perimeter is monotone under inclusion of convex sets, and $\operatorname{per}Q\le\pi\operatorname{diam}Q$ (Cauchy's formula)~\cite{Schneider}.
\item \emph{Steinhagen's inequality}~\cite{Steinhagen}: every planar convex body has minimal width $w\le3\,r_{\mathrm{in}}$, where $r_{\mathrm{in}}$ is its inradius.
\item \emph{Non-interlacing} (a consequence of the Jordan curve theorem, see~\cite{MoharThomassen}): if $Q$ is a closed topological disc and $a,b,a',b'$ lie on $\partial Q$ in this cyclic order, then every path in $Q$ from $a$ to $a'$ meets every path in $Q$ from $b$ to $b'$.
\item \emph{Monotone maps of circles.} A continuous surjection between two circles whose fibres are connected is monotone: preimages of arcs are arcs, and the images of the arcs between cyclically consecutive points of a finite set tile the target circle.
\item If $|A|\ge2$, the boundary arcs of $\partial K$ between cyclically consecutive points of $A$ have length at least the chord, hence at least $1$. So $|A|\le\operatorname{per}K+1$, and an arc of length $\ell$ contains at most $\ell+1$ points of $A$. Also $\operatorname{per}K\le2\pi\Dt$, since $K\subseteq\Dd(w,\Dt)$.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Facts about $K$.} For $z\in K$ put $\operatorname{depth}(z):=\dist(z,\R^2\setminus K)=\min_{w\in X}(\Dt-|z-w|)$. Depth is $1$-Lipschitz, and $\operatorname{depth}(z)\ge\eta$ if and only if $\Dd(z,\eta)\subseteq K$. Since $\partial K$ is a finite union of arcs of circles $\Cc(w,\Dt)$, $K$ is strictly convex: a segment between two points of $K$ meets $\partial K$ at most in its endpoints. If $\operatorname{int}K=\emptyset$ then $|Y|\le1$ and there is nothing to prove; from now on $\operatorname{int}K\neq\emptyset$.

\begin{lemma}[fatness]\label{lem:fat}
If $\Delta\le1.94\,\Dt$ and $\operatorname{int}K\neq\emptyset$, then $\operatorname{diam}K\le100\,w(K)$, and hence $r_{\mathrm{in}}(K)\ge\operatorname{diam}K/300$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let the minimal width $w_0$ of $K$ be attained in the direction $u_0$, let $v_0\perp u_0$, and let $L$ be the width in the direction $v_0$. Then $\operatorname{diam}K\le\sqrt{w_0^2+L^2}$, and we may assume $L\ge3w_0$. Let $p,q\in K$ be extreme in the directions $-v_0$ and $v_0$, and $m:=(p+q)/2$. The line $m+\R u_0$ meets $K$ in a segment $[y_-,y_+]$ of length at most $w_0$; write $y_+=m+s\,u_0$ with $0\le s\le w_0$. As $y_+\in\partial K$, we have $y_+\in\Cc(x_+,\Dt)$ for some $x_+\in X$; put $n_+:=(y_+-x_+)/\Dt$. Every $k\in K\subseteq\Dd(x_+,\Dt)$ satisfies $(k-y_+)\cdot n_+\le-|k-y_+|^2/(2\Dt)$. Adding this for $k=p$ and $k=q$ gives $-2s\,u_0\cdot n_+<0$, so $u_0\cdot n_+>0$; using the two inequalities separately gives $|(p-m)\cdot n_+|\le s\,u_0\cdot n_+\le w_0$, so $|(q-p)\cdot n_+|\le2w_0$. Writing $q-p=Lv_0+\sigma u_0$ with $|\sigma|\le w_0$, we get $|v_0\cdot n_+|\le3w_0/L$ and $u_0\cdot n_+\ge\sqrt{1-9w_0^2/L^2}$. The same argument at $y_-$ gives $x_-$ with $u_0\cdot n_-\le-\sqrt{1-9w_0^2/L^2}$. Hence
\[
1.94\,\Dt\ge|x_+-x_-|\ge(x_--x_+)\cdot u_0\ge2\Dt\sqrt{1-9w_0^2/L^2}-w_0 .
\]
If $w_0\le0.02\Dt$, this gives $\sqrt{1-9w_0^2/L^2}\le0.98$, so $L/w_0\le3/\sqrt{0.0396}<15.1$ and $\operatorname{diam}K<\sqrt{1+15.1^2}\,w_0<100w_0$. Otherwise $\operatorname{diam}K\le2\Dt<100w_0$. The last claim follows from (P5).\chk{E}
\end{proof}
(Numerically, the largest aspect ratio $\operatorname{diam}K/w(K)$ seems to be about $8.1$, attained by the lens with centre distance $1.94\,\Dt$.)

\subsection{Proof of \texorpdfstring{\cref{thm:Eprime}}{Theorem E'}}
\emph{Step 0 (parameters).} Put $\rho_0:=\lfloor\min(n^{1/3},\Dt/400)\rfloor$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item[(F1)] If $\rho_0=0$, then $\Dt<400$, so $\Delta<776$ and $n\le(2\Delta+1)^2<1553^2<135^3$. Then $2|A|\le2n\le270\,n^{2/3}$, and $\operatorname{Def}\ge0$ proves the claim.
\end{itemize}
Otherwise $\rho_0\ge1$. For $\rho\in\{\rho_0,\dots,2\rho_0-1\}$ the shells $Y_\rho:=\{y\in Y:\rho\le\operatorname{depth}(y)<\rho+1\}$ are disjoint; fix $\rho$ with $|Y_\rho|\le n/\rho_0$ and put $\eta:=\rho+\frac12$. Then $\frac32\le\eta\le2\rho_0-\frac12<2n^{1/3}$ and $\eta<\Dt/200$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item[(F2)] If $r_{\mathrm{in}}(K)\le\eta$, then by \cref{lem:fat}, (P4) and (P8), $|A|\le\operatorname{per}K+1\le300\pi\eta+1$, so $2|A|\le1200\pi\,n^{1/3}+2\le3772\,n^{2/3}$.
\item[(F3)] If $\operatorname{per}K<100\eta$, then $2|A|\le200\eta+2\le402\,n^{2/3}$.
\end{itemize}
From now on $r_{\mathrm{in}}(K)>\eta$ and $\operatorname{per}K\ge100\eta$.

\emph{Step 1 (shell removal).} Put $Y_{\mathrm{out}}:=\{y\in Y:\operatorname{depth}y<\rho\}$, $Y_{\mathrm{in}}:=\{y\in Y:\operatorname{depth}y\ge\rho+1\}$ and $H_{\mathrm{out}}:=G[Y_{\mathrm{out}}]$. Depth is $1$-Lipschitz, so no edge joins $Y_{\mathrm{out}}$ to $Y_{\mathrm{in}}$. At most $6|Y_\rho|$ edges meet $Y_\rho$, and $\operatorname{Def}(G[Y_{\mathrm{in}}])\ge0$; hence $\operatorname{Def}(G[Y])\ge\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})-3|Y_\rho|\ge\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})-3n/\rho_0$. Also $A\subseteq Y_{\mathrm{out}}$, and we may assume $A\neq\emptyset$.

\emph{Step 2 (the core $K'$ and the retraction $P$).} Let $K':=\{z\in K:\operatorname{depth}z\ge\eta\}=\bigcap_w\Dd(w,\Dt-\eta)$; it is compact and convex, with nonempty interior since the incentre of $K$ has depth $r_{\mathrm{in}}>\eta$. Every point of the drawing of $H_{\mathrm{out}}$ has depth less than $\rho+\frac12=\eta$, since a point on a unit edge is within $\frac12$ of an endpoint. So $K'$ misses the drawing and lies in a single face $F_0$ of $H_{\mathrm{out}}$.

$\partial K'$ consists of finitely many open smooth arcs, on circles $\Cc(w,\Dt-\eta)$, and finitely many corners $c$. At a corner, the normal cone $N_{K'}(c)$ is the cone between the unit normals $\nu_i=(c-w_i)/(\Dt-\eta)$, $i=1,2$, for two centres $w_1,w_2\in X$; let $\psi_c\in(0,\pi)$ be its angle. Normal cones of distinct corners have disjoint interiors, so $\sum_c\psi_c\le2\pi$.

Let $P:\partial K\to\partial K'$ be the nearest-point map onto $K'$, restricted to $\partial K$. If $P(x)=y$ then $x\in y+N_{K'}(y)$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Offset points.} If $y$ lies on a smooth arc of $\Cc(w,\Dt-\eta)$ with normal $\nu=(y-w)/(\Dt-\eta)$, then the open disc $B(y,\eta)$ lies in $\operatorname{int}K$ and $|y+\eta\nu-w|=\Dt$, so the ray $y+\R_+\nu$ leaves $K$ exactly at $y+\eta\nu$. Hence $P^{-1}(y)=\{y+\eta\nu\}$; on each smooth arc, $P^{-1}$ is the dilation about $w$ with factor $\Dt/(\Dt-\eta)$.
\item \emph{Collapse arcs.} At a corner, $Z_c:=P^{-1}(c)=\partial K\cap(c+N_{K'}(c))$ is a closed arc of $\partial K$; distinct $Z_c$ are disjoint.
\end{itemize}
$P$ is continuous and surjective (the ray from $y\in\partial K'$ along an outer normal leaves $K$ at a point $x$ with $P(x)=y$), and its fibres are points or arcs $Z_c$, hence connected. By (P7), $P$ is monotone. Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{eq:perKprime}
\operatorname{per}K=\frac{\Dt}{\Dt-\eta}\operatorname{per}K'+\sum_c\operatorname{len}Z_c,
\end{equation}
and, writing $\operatorname{col}(\alpha):=\operatorname{len}(\alpha\cap\bigcup_cZ_c)$ for an arc $\alpha\subseteq\partial K$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lenP}
\operatorname{len}P(\alpha)\ge\Bigl(1-\frac{\eta}{\Dt}\Bigr)\bigl(\operatorname{len}\alpha-\operatorname{col}(\alpha)\bigr).
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 3 (collapse length).} The points $p_i:=c+\eta\nu_i$ lie in $\Dd(c,\eta)\subseteq K$ and on $\Cc(w_i,\Dt)$, so they lie on $\partial K$ and are the endpoints of $Z_c$. The convex set $K\cap(c+N_{K'}(c))$ has perimeter $2\eta+\operatorname{len}Z_c$. Since $K$ lies behind the tangent line to $\Cc(w_i,\Dt)$ at $p_i$, this set lies in the kite with vertices $c$, $p_1$, $p_2$ and the intersection of the two tangent lines, which has right angles at $p_1,p_2$ and perimeter $2\eta+2\eta\tan(\psi_c/2)$. By (P4), $\operatorname{len}Z_c\le2\eta\tan(\psi_c/2)$. Also $|w_1-w_2|=2(\Dt-\eta)\sin(\psi_c/2)\le1.94\,\Dt$, so $\sin(\psi_c/2)\le0.97/(1-1/200)=194/199$. With $\theta^*:=\arcsin(194/199)>1.343$ and $\tan\theta^*=194/\sqrt{1965}\le3.26\,\theta^*$, and since $\tan x/x$ is increasing on $(0,\pi/2)$, we get $\tan(\psi_c/2)\le1.63\,\psi_c$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:collapse}
\sum_c\operatorname{len}Z_c\le3.26\,\eta\sum_c\psi_c\le3.26\cdot2\pi\,\eta<21\eta .
\end{equation}
By~\eqref{eq:perKprime} and $\operatorname{per}K\le2\pi\Dt$, $\operatorname{per}K'\ge\operatorname{per}K-(21+2\pi)\eta$; also $\operatorname{per}K'\ge\frac{199}{200}\cdot79\eta>2$ by (F3).\chk{E}

\emph{Step 4 (case (i): $F_0$ is bounded).} In (P2) keep only the outer face and $F_0$:
\[
\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})\ge\sum_{j\ \mathrm{outer}}(3+|w_j|)+|F_0|-3 .
\]
Every point of $A$ lies on the outer face (the outer normal ray from it leaves $K$ at once, and the drawing lies in $K$), hence on the outer walk of its component; write $A_j:=A\cap C_j$. If $|A_j|\ge2$, then $w_j$ is a closed curve through the points of $A_j$, which are in convex position, so $|w_j|\ge\operatorname{per}\conv A_j\ge|A_j|$; if $|A_j|\le1$ then $3+|w_j|\ge|A_j|$. The boundary of $F_0$ separates $K'$ from $\infty$, so $|F_0|\ge\operatorname{per}K'$ by (P3). With Step~3 and $\operatorname{per}K\ge|A|-1$,
\[
\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})\ge2|A|-(21+2\pi)\eta-4 .
\]

\begin{lemma}[winding]\label{lem:winding}
Suppose $F_0$ is the outer face of $H_{\mathrm{out}}$. Let $C_j$ be a component with $k:=|A_j|\ge2$, list $A_j$ in cyclic order on $\partial K$, and let $\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_k$ be the arcs of $\partial K$ between consecutive points. Then $|w_j|\ge2\sum_{i\neq i^*}\operatorname{len}P(\alpha_i)$, where $\alpha_{i^*}$ is an arc with the largest image.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix $o\in\operatorname{int}K'$, and let $\gamma:[0,L]\to\R^2$, $L=|w_j|$, be the closed unit-speed polygonal curve tracing $w_j$. The outer face of $H_{\mathrm{out}}$ lies in the outer face of $C_j$, which is a connected unbounded open set disjoint from the image of $\gamma$; it contains $K'\ni o$. So $o$ is in the unbounded component of $\R^2\setminus\gamma([0,L])$, and the winding number of $\gamma$ about $o$ is $0$. For $x\notin\operatorname{int}K'$, the projection inequality $(x-Px)\cdot(o-Px)\le0$ gives $(x-o)\cdot(Px-o)\ge|Px-o|^2>0$; so $x-o$ and $Px-o$ are never opposite, the straight-line homotopy from $\gamma$ to $P\circ\gamma$ avoids $o$, and $P\circ\gamma$ also has winding number $0$ about $o$. Radial projection from $o$ is a homeomorphism $\partial K'\to S^1$, so $P\circ\gamma$ has degree $0$ as a map into $\partial K'\cong\R/(\operatorname{per}K')\mathbb Z$. Let $E:\R\to\partial K'$ be the arc-length covering map; degree $0$ means that a lift $\beta$ of $P\circ\gamma$ is a closed curve in $\R$. As $E$ preserves length and $P$ is $1$-Lipschitz, $\operatorname{length}(\beta)\le L$. A closed curve in $\R$ with image $[m,M]$ has length at least $2(M-m)$. Every point of $A_j$ is visited by $\gamma$, so $E([m,M])$ is an arc of $\partial K'$ (or all of $\partial K'$) containing all $P(a)$, $a\in A_j$. The arcs $P(\alpha_i)$ tile $\partial K'$ between consecutive points $P(a)$ (P7), so the complementary open arc of $E([m,M])$ lies inside a single $P(\alpha_i)$. Hence $M-m\ge\sum_{i\neq i^*}\operatorname{len}P(\alpha_i)$.
\end{proof}

\emph{Step 5 (case (ii): $F_0$ is the outer face).} Every bounded face has $|f|\ge3$, so $\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})\ge\sum_{j\ \mathrm{outer}}(3+|w_j|)$. For a gap arc $\alpha$ put $e(\alpha):=\max\{0,1-\operatorname{len}P(\alpha)\}$. If $|A_j|\ge2$, \cref{lem:winding} gives $|w_j|\ge2\sum_{i\neq i^*}(1-e(\alpha_i))\ge2(|A_j|-1)-2\sum_ie(\alpha_i)$, so $3+|w_j|\ge2|A_j|-2\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{gaps}(j)}e(\alpha)$; if $|A_j|\le1$ then $3+|w_j|\ge2|A_j|$. Summing,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:E51}
\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})\ge2|A|-2\sum_\alpha e(\alpha),
\end{equation}
over all gap arcs of all components. Since $\operatorname{len}\alpha\ge1$,~\eqref{eq:lenP} gives $e(\alpha)\le\eta/\Dt+\operatorname{col}(\alpha)$. There are at most $|A|$ gap arcs, and $|A|\,\eta/\Dt\le(2\pi\Dt+1)\eta/\Dt\le2\pi\eta+\frac1{200}$ by (P8). Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:E52}
\sum_\alpha e(\alpha)\le2\pi\eta+\tfrac1{200}+\sum_{\alpha:\,e(\alpha)>0}\operatorname{col}(\alpha).
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 6 (corner multiplicity).} Fix a corner $c$, and let $\mathcal F_c$ be the set of gap arcs $\alpha$, over all components, with $e(\alpha)>0$ and $\alpha\cap Z_c\neq\emptyset$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Window.} Let $B_c$ be the set of points of $\partial K'$ at arc distance less than $1$ from $c$. Since $\operatorname{per}K'>2$, $B_c$ is a proper open arc of length at most $2$; let $U_c:=P^{-1}(B_c)$, a proper open arc of $\partial K$. If $\alpha\in\mathcal F_c$, then $P(\alpha)$ is an arc of length less than $1$ containing $c$, so $P(\alpha)\subseteq B_c$ and $\alpha\subseteq U_c$. The arc $U_c$ consists of the offset preimage of $B_c$ minus its corners, together with the collapse arcs $Z_{c'}$ of all corners $c'\in B_c$; so $\operatorname{len}U_c\le2\Dt/(\Dt-\eta)+\sum_{c'}\operatorname{len}Z_{c'}\le21\eta+3$, and $U_c$ contains at most $N:=\lfloor21\eta+4\rfloor$ points of $A$ (P8).
\item \emph{Laminarity.} $U_c$ is a proper arc, hence linearly ordered, and each $\alpha\in\mathcal F_c$ is an interval of $U_c$ with endpoints in $A\cap U_c$. Two arcs of $\mathcal F_c$ from the same component are distinct gaps of one cyclic list, so they have disjoint interiors. Two arcs from different components $C_j$, $C_k$ have four distinct endpoints; if they interlaced, then by (P6) a path in $C_j$ joining the endpoints of the first and a path in $C_k$ joining the endpoints of the second would meet, although distinct components are disjoint as point sets (P1). So $\mathcal F_c$ is a laminar family of distinct intervals with endpoints in an $N$-point set, and such a family has at most $2N-3\le42\eta+5$ members (map an interval to the set of elementary segments it covers). The restriction to arcs with $e(\alpha)>0$ matters here: a component nested inside a gap of another has a ``back'' gap arc that runs around the rest of $\partial K$, and such arcs, which are not contained in $U_c$, can interlace with others.
\end{itemize}
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:E61}
\sum_{\alpha:\,e(\alpha)>0}\operatorname{col}(\alpha)=\sum_c\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal F_c}\operatorname{len}(\alpha\cap Z_c)\le(42\eta+5)\sum_c\operatorname{len}Z_c\le21\eta(42\eta+5).
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 7 (assembly).} In case (ii),~\eqref{eq:E51}, \eqref{eq:E52} and~\eqref{eq:E61} give $\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})\ge2|A|-1764\eta^2-(210+4\pi)\eta-\frac1{100}$. With case (i),
\[
\operatorname{Def}(H_{\mathrm{out}})\ge2|A|-E(\eta),\qquad E(\eta):=1764\eta^2+223\eta+4\le7506\,n^{2/3},
\]
using $\eta<2n^{1/3}$. The shell cost $3n/\rho_0$ is at most $6n^{2/3}$ if $\rho_0=\lfloor n^{1/3}\rfloor\ge n^{1/3}/2$. If $\rho_0=\lfloor\Dt/400\rfloor\ge\Dt/800$, it is at most $2400n/\Dt$; from $n\le(2\Delta+1)^2$ and $\Delta\le1.94\Dt$ we get $\Dt\ge\sqrt n/7.76$ for $n\ge4$, so the cost is at most $18624\sqrt n\le18624\,n^{2/3}$, and for $n<4$ it is at most $12$. Altogether $\operatorname{Def}(G[Y])\ge2|A|-(7506+18636)\,n^{2/3}\ge2|A|-3\cdot10^4\,n^{2/3}$, and the cases (F1)--(F3) cost at most $3772\,n^{2/3}$.\chk{E} \qed

\subsection{The bound for \texorpdfstring{$\tau>1$}{tau > 1}}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:tau1}
In the nondegenerate regime with $\tau>1$,
\[
\min\{\mult(\Dt),\mult(\delta)\}\le\tfrac97n+\tfrac37\bigl(3\cdot10^4\,n^{2/3}+3.4\cdot10^4\bigr).
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Recall (\cref{sec:reductions}) that in the nondegenerate regime we may assume $\Dt\ge10^4$, which the count of bad DD edges in \cref{sec:bad} requires. Since $\tau>1$ if and only if $t>1$, \cref{lem:ballpoly}(c) gives $\dist(D,K)\ge t>1$, while $X\setminus D\subseteq K$; so no $\delta$-edge joins $D$ to $X\setminus D$, and $\operatorname{Def}(G)=\operatorname{Def}(G[D])+\operatorname{Def}(G[X\setminus D])$.

\emph{The part on $D$.} The definitions of \cref{sec:setup} for $S\cap D$ extend verbatim to all of $D$: fix a diametral partner $x_z$ of each $z\in D$ and put $u_z:=(x_z-z)/\Delta$; \cref{lem:identities}(a) holds for every $z\in D$; call a $\delta$-edge inside $D$ good if $\angle(u_z,u_{z'})\le\beta$. On a good edge, \cref{lem:identities}(a) at both ends gives $a,b>0$, and $|a+b|\le\beta$ then gives $a,b\in(0,\beta)$, so it is a T-edge; by (T1) there are at most $|D|$ of them. The count of DD bad edges in \cref{sec:bad} uses only that points of $D$ are vertices of $P$ and the lenses $\Dd(x_z,\Delta)\cap\Dd(x_{z'},\Delta)$, so it applies to all of $D$ and gives fewer than $3.4\cdot10^4$ bad edges inside $D$ (or at most $6\cdot1203$ edges inside $D$ if the perimeter of $P$ is below $1202$). Hence $\operatorname{Def}(G[D])\ge2|D|-3.4\cdot10^4\ge2|S\cap D|-3.4\cdot10^4$.

\emph{The part in $K$} is \cref{thm:Eprime}: $\operatorname{Def}(G[X\setminus D])\ge2|S\setminus D|-3\cdot10^4\,n^{2/3}$.

So $\mult(\delta)=3n-\operatorname{Def}(G)\le3n-2s+3\cdot10^4\,n^{2/3}+3.4\cdot10^4$, while $\mult(\Dt)\le\frac32s$ by \cref{lem:VS}. The combination with weights $\frac47$ and $\frac37$ gives the claim, since $\frac47\cdot\frac32s+\frac37(3n-2s)=\frac97n$.\chk{E}
\end{proof}

\emph{Proof of Theorem 1(c).} In the degenerate regime the bound is $n+1\le\frac97n+n^{2/3}$. In the nondegenerate regime with $\Dt<10^4$ we have $n<N_0$, and $3n\le\frac97n+\frac{12}7N_0^{1/3}n^{2/3}<\frac97n+2000\,n^{2/3}$. Otherwise \cref{cor:tau1} gives at most $\frac97n+\frac37(3\cdot10^4+3.4\cdot10^4)\,n^{2/3}<\frac97n+3\cdot10^4\,n^{2/3}$. So Theorem~1(c) holds with $C_2=3\cdot10^4$. \qed

\begin{remark}\label{rem:Eprime}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em,label=(\alph*)]
\item Theorem~1(c) matches the global lower bound $L\ge\frac97$, in the sense that sets with $\tau(X)>1$ cannot do better than $\frac97$. It does not show that $\frac97$ is attained by sets with $\tau(X)>1$: the constructions of~\cite{ienjoymath} have $t<1$ and lie in Region~I.
\item The argument is global. The first layer alone does not pay: there are exact configurations (checked in $60$-digit arithmetic, scripts \texttt{r6\_gap\_L\_family.py} and \texttt{r6\_gap\_M\_pairgap.py}) in which the edges near $\partial K$ do not give a deficiency of $2$ per point of $A$. The missing amount is paid by a second closed walk: the boundary of the hole $F_0$ in case~(i), or the return path of \cref{lem:winding} in case~(ii).
\item \Cref{thm:Eprime} holds for every $\tau$. What prevents the same conclusion for $\tau\le1$ is the edges between $D$ and $X\setminus D$ (the rungs and the D-neighbours of points of $R$), which are absent when $\tau>1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{A layer bound and a new class of sets}\label{sec:layers}

In this section $X$ is an arbitrary finite planar set with at least two distinct distances, $\Delta>\Dt$ are its two largest distances, and we scale so that $\Dt=1$. Every distance of $X$ is at most $1$ or equal to $\Delta$. $L_1$ is the set of extreme points of $X$ (vertices of $\conv X$), $L_2$ is the set of extreme points of $X\setminus L_1$, and $m_3:=n-|L_1|-|L_2|$. Let $G$ be the graph on $L_1\cup L_2$ whose edges are the $\Dt$-pairs, let $G'$ be its $2$-core (repeatedly delete vertices of degree less than $2$), and write $L_i':=L_i\cap V(G')$ and $\deg'=\deg_{G'}$. For $p\in X$ let $h(p)$ be the number of points of $X$ at distance $\Dt$ from $p$.

\begin{lemma}[location]\label{lem:location}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item Both endpoints of every $\Delta$-pair lie in $L_1$.
\item Every $\Dt$-pair has an endpoint in $L_1$ and both endpoints in $L_1\cup L_2$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular $G$ contains every $\Dt$-pair of $X$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) If $|pq|=\Delta$ then $X\subseteq\Dd(q,\Delta)$ and $p$ lies on its boundary circle, so $p$ is an extreme point of $X$. (ii) Let $|pq|=1$. If $p\notin L_1$, then $p$ has no $\Delta$-partner by (i), so $X\subseteq\Dd(p,1)$ and $q$, lying on the boundary circle, is in $L_1$. So some endpoint, say $p$, lies in $L_1$. Suppose $q\notin L_1$. Every point $x$ with $|px|>1$ has $|px|=\Delta$ and so lies in $L_1$. Hence $X\setminus L_1\subseteq\Dd(p,1)$ with $q$ on the boundary circle, so $q$ is extreme in $X\setminus L_1$, i.e.\ $q\in L_2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[core degree of $L_2$]\label{prop:core2}
Every $q\in L_2'$ has $\deg'(q)=2$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Put $q=0$. Since $q$ is not extreme it has no $\Delta$-partner (\cref{lem:location}(i)), so $X\subseteq\Dd(0,1)$ and the $G$-neighbours of $q$ lie on the unit circle. Since $q\in V(G')$, $\deg'(q)\ge2$. Suppose $v_1,v_2,v_3$ are three distinct $G'$-neighbours of $q$. Each $|v_iv_j|$ is either at most $1$ or equal to $\Delta$. The argument follows the three cases of Vesztergombi's inner-point analysis~\cite[pp.~192--194]{Ve87}, with one change: we delete no points, and the contradiction in Cases~1 and~2 is with membership in the $2$-core, via $\deg_G\ge\deg'$.

\emph{Case 3: at most one pair $v_iv_j$ at distance $\Delta$.} Label so that $|v_1v_2|,|v_2v_3|\le1$. Then $v_2$ lies on the shorter arc between $v_1$ and $v_3$, and in polar angle we may take $v_2$ at $0$, $v_1$ at $a$ and $v_3$ at $-b$ with $0<a,b\le60^\circ$. Since $q$ is not extreme, the closed half-plane $\{x:x\cdot v_2\le0\}$ contains a point $t\in X$, $t\ne q$. For $t\ne0$ with $t\cdot v_2\le0$ we get $|t-v_2|^2=|t|^2-2t\cdot v_2+1>1$, so $|tv_2|=\Delta$. By symmetry assume the polar angle $\varphi$ of $t$ lies in $[90^\circ,180^\circ]$. Then the angle between $t$ and $v_3$ is $\varphi+b\in(90^\circ,240^\circ]$, so $t\cdot v_3<0$ and $|tv_3|=\Delta$. Hence $t$ lies on the perpendicular bisector of $v_2v_3$, which passes through $0$, so $\varphi=180^\circ-b/2$. The angle between $t$ and $v_1$ is then $180^\circ-b/2-a\ge90^\circ$, so $|tv_1|^2\ge|t|^2+1>1$ and $|tv_1|=\Delta$. Now $v_1,v_2,v_3$ lie on both $\Cc(0,1)$ and $\Cc(t,\Delta)$, two distinct circles, which meet in at most two points. Contradiction. (This case uses only that $q$ is not extreme.)

\emph{Case 2: exactly two pairs at $\Delta$.} Say $|v_1v_2|=|v_1v_3|=\Delta$ and $|v_2v_3|\le1$. Then $v_1$ lies on the perpendicular bisector of $v_2v_3$, and we may take $v_1=(-1,0)$, $v_{2,3}=(\cos a,\pm\sin a)$ with $0<a\le30^\circ$ and $\Delta=2\cos(a/2)$. We claim $v_1$ has no $\Dt$-neighbour $r\ne q$; then $\deg_G(v_1)=1$, contradicting $v_1\in V(G')$. Such an $r=(x,y)$ satisfies $|r-v_1|=1$ (so $|r|^2=-2x$), $|r|\le1$, and $|r-v_i|\in[0,1]\cup\{\Delta\}$ for $i=2,3$; by symmetry $y\ge0$. Write $c=\cos a$, $s_a=\sin a$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Both $|r-v_2|,|r-v_3|\le1$.} Impossible: $|r-v_3|^2-1=-2x(1+c)+2ys_a\ge0$, with equality only if $r=q$.
\item \emph{$|r-v_2|=\Delta$} (the case $|r-v_3|=\Delta$ is symmetric). Using $|r|^2=-2x$, this condition is the linear equation $-2x(1+c)-2s_ay=1+2c$. Intersecting with the circle $|r-v_1|=1$ gives $x=\bigl(\pm s_a\sqrt{8c+7}-3(1+c)\bigr)/(4(1+c))$, so $|r|^2=\bigl(3(1+c)\mp s_a\sqrt{8c+7}\bigr)/(2(1+c))$. The root with $+s_a\sqrt{8c+7}$ in $|r|^2$ has $|r|^2\ge3/2$, outside the disc. For the other root, $|r|^2\le1\iff1+c\le s_a\sqrt{8c+7}\iff1+c\le(1-c)(8c+7)\iff c^2\le\frac34\iff a\ge30^\circ$. So only $a=30^\circ$ remains, and there $|r-v_3|^2=3+c-s_a\sqrt{8c+7}=2$, which lies strictly between $1$ and $\Delta^2=2+\sqrt3$. Forbidden.
\item \emph{Both equal $\Delta$.} Then $r$ lies on the $x$-axis, so $r\in\{(0,0),(-2,0)\}$, and $(-2,0)$ is outside the disc.
\end{itemize}

\emph{Case 1: all three pairs at $\Delta$.} Then $v_1,v_2,v_3$ form an equilateral triangle with circumcentre $q$, and $\Delta=\sqrt3$. Let $P\ne q$ be a further $\Dt$-neighbour of some $v_i$, so $|P-v_i|=1$ and $|P|\le1$. It cannot have both other distances at most $1$: $|P-v_i|=1$ gives $|P|^2=2P\cdot v_i$, the two conditions give $P\cdot(v_i-v_j)\le0$ for $j\ne i$, and adding and using $\sum_jv_j=0$ gives $3P\cdot v_i\le0$, so $P=q$. Hence $|P-v_j|=\sqrt3$ for some $j\ne i$. Solving exactly, there are six candidate points, two for each $v_i$, each with $|P|^2=(9-\sqrt{33})/6$ and at squared distance $(7-\sqrt{33})/2\approx0.63$ from the third vertex, so each is a $\Dt$-neighbour of exactly one $v_i$. Their pairwise squared distances are $(29-5\sqrt{33})/6\approx0.046$ for three disjoint pairs (each joining candidates of different vertices), and otherwise $4/3$, $(9-\sqrt{33})/2\approx1.628$ or $(17-\sqrt{33})/6\approx1.876$, all strictly inside the forbidden interval $(1,3)$. So a set of candidates that can coexist in $X$ has at most two elements, at most two of the $v_i$ have a second $\Dt$-neighbour, and some $v_i$ has $\deg_G(v_i)=1$, contradicting $v_i\in V(G')$.

With four or more neighbours apply the argument to any three. Hence $\deg'(q)\le2$, so $\deg'(q)=2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[opposite-side lemma]\label{lem:M}
Let $|pa|=|pb|=|br|=1$ with $r\neq p$. Suppose $a$ lies strictly on one side of the line $pb$ and $r$ does not lie strictly on that side. Then $|ar|>1$, so $|ar|=\Delta$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $p=0$, $b=(1,0)$, $a=(\cos A,-\sin A)$ with $0<A<\pi$, and $r=b+(\cos F,\sin F)$ with $0\le F\le\pi$; $F\ne\pi$ as $r\ne p$. Then $|r-a|^2-1=2(1+\cos F)(1-\cos A)+2\sin F\sin A$, where the first term is positive and the second nonnegative.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:F}
Let $p$ be an extreme point of $X$ with $\Dt$-neighbours $w_1,\dots,w_k$ in angular order. Then $\deg_G(w_i)=1$ for every $3\le i\le k-2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Since $p$ is extreme, some line through $p$ has $X\setminus\{p\}$ strictly on one side. So the directions of the $w_j$ from $p$ are distinct, lie in an open half-plane, and any two differ by less than $180^\circ$; for $j<i<l$, $w_j$ and $w_l$ lie strictly on opposite sides of the line $pw_i$. Suppose $r\ne p$ and $|rw_i|=1$. Put $p=0$, $w_i=(1,0)$. Then $w_{i-2},w_{i-1}$ lie strictly below the $x$-axis and $w_{i+1},w_{i+2}$ strictly above; by symmetry assume $r$ is not strictly below. Write $w_{i-2}=e(-\alpha_1)$, $w_{i-1}=e(-\alpha_2)$ with $0<\alpha_2<\alpha_1<180^\circ$. By \cref{lem:M}, $|rw_{i-2}|=|rw_{i-1}|=\Delta$, so $r$ lies on the perpendicular bisector of $w_{i-2}w_{i-1}$, the line through $0$ in direction $-\gamma$ with $\gamma=(\alpha_1+\alpha_2)/2\in(0^\circ,180^\circ)$. As $r\ne0$ is not below the axis, $r=\lambda e(180^\circ-\gamma)$ with $\lambda>0$. From $|r-w_i|=1$ we get $\lambda(\lambda+2\cos\gamma)=0$, so $\lambda=-2\cos\gamma$, whence $\gamma>90^\circ$ and $\alpha_1>90^\circ$. Then $|w_{i-2}w_i|=2\sin(\alpha_1/2)>\sqrt2>1$, so $|w_{i-2}w_i|=\Delta$. Let $\theta>0$ be the direction of $w_{i+1}$; then $\alpha_1+\theta<180^\circ$, and since the chord length $2\sin(x/2)$ is strictly increasing on $[0^\circ,180^\circ]$, $|w_{i-2}w_{i+1}|=2\sin((\alpha_1+\theta)/2)>2\sin(\alpha_1/2)=\Delta$, contradicting the maximality of $\Delta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[degree at most $4$]\label{thm:deg4}
Every $p\in L_1'$ has $\deg'(p)\le4$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $p\in L_1'$ has five $G'$-neighbours $u_1,\dots,u_5$ in angular order. Among the $G$-neighbours of $p$, $u_3$ has at least two on each side, so $u_3=w_i$ with $3\le i\le k-2$, and $\deg_G(u_3)=1$ by \cref{lem:F}. But $u_3\in V(G')$ forces $\deg_G(u_3)\ge2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[layer bound]\label{thm:layer}
For every finite $X\subset\R^2$ with at least two distinct distances,
\[
\mult(\Dt)\ \le\ |L_1|+|L_2|+\tfrac12\sum_{p\in L_1'}\bigl(\deg'(p)-2\bigr).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:location}, $\mult(\Dt)=e(G)$. Each deletion made while pruning $G$ to its $2$-core removes at most one edge, so $e(G)\le e(G')+|V(G)\setminus V(G')|$. By \cref{prop:core2}, $e(G')=\frac12\sum_{v\in V(G')}\deg'(v)=|V(G')|+\frac12\sum_{p\in L_1'}(\deg'(p)-2)$. Add the two estimates and use $|V(G)|=|L_1|+|L_2|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:A1}
Assume \textup{(H3)}: no point of $L_1$ has four or more points of $X$ at distance $\Dt$. (This holds, for instance, if no four points of $X$ are concyclic.) Then
\[
\mult(\Dt)\le|L_1|+|L_2|+\tfrac12\#\{p\in L_1':\deg'(p)=3\}\le\tfrac32|L_1|+|L_2|.
\]
Consequently, under \textup{(H3)}, if $|L_1|\le2m_3$ then $\Delta$ and $\Dt$ are two distinct distances each occurring at least once and at most $n$ times.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Under (H3), $2\le\deg'(p)\le h(p)\le3$ for $p\in L_1'$. The condition $\frac32|L_1|+|L_2|\le n$ is equivalent to $|L_1|\le2m_3$, and $\mult(\Delta)\le n$ by~\cite{HP34}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[unconditional form]\label{cor:uncond}
For every finite $X$ with at least two distances,
$\mult(\Dt)\le|L_1|+|L_2|+\frac12\sum_{p\in L_1}(\min\{h(p),4\}-2)^+$. Hence $\mult(\Dt)\le n$ whenever $\sum_{p\in L_1}(\min\{h(p),4\}-2)^+\le2m_3$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Combine \cref{thm:layer} with $2\le\deg'(p)\le\min\{h(p),4\}$ on $L_1'$ (\cref{thm:deg4}).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:layers}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em,label=(\alph*)]
\item \Cref{cor:A1} covers sets not covered by~\eqref{eq:cdl13}. For example, if $|L_1|=|L_2|=0.4n$ and $m_3=0.2n$, the three quantities in~\eqref{eq:cdl13} are $1.2n$, $1.33n$ and $1.2n$, while \cref{cor:A1} gives $\mult(\Dt)\le n$ (under (H3)). Of course~\eqref{eq:cdl13} needs no hypothesis like (H3).
\item Using $\deg'\le4$, \cref{thm:layer} recovers the bound $2|L_1|+|L_2|$ of~\cite[Theorem~1.3]{CDL25}. \Cref{prop:core2} and \cref{thm:deg4} are the inputs from~\cite{Ve96} (Propositions~3 and~5--8 there, as used in~\cite{CDL25}) needed for this; we prove them directly in the $2$-core, without deleting points. We have not checked the other inputs used in~\cite{CDL25} for the first two bounds in~\eqref{eq:cdl13}.
\item In \cref{cor:A1}, the bound $\frac32|L_1|+|L_2|$ is attained under (H3) alone by the following example, which we have checked numerically for $m=8,12,16$ (floating point): a regular $m$-gon with $m$ even, with a point at distance $\Dt$ from both endpoints of every other edge (CDL's ``servers''). These examples contain concyclic quadruples; for sets with no four concyclic points see \cref{q:genericity} below.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[distinct middle neighbours]\label{prop:middle}
Let $p\ne p'$ be points of $X$ with a common $\Dt$-neighbour $w$, and suppose that each of $p,p'$ has $\Dt$-neighbours strictly on both sides of its line to $w$. Then $X$ contains four concyclic points. In particular, if no four points of $X$ are concyclic, then distinct vertices of $\conv X$ with three $\Dt$-neighbours have distinct middle $\Dt$-neighbours.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The point $p'$ is not strictly on both sides of the line $pw$, so choose a $\Dt$-neighbour $a$ of $p$ strictly on the side of $pw$ on which $p'$ does not lie strictly. \Cref{lem:M} (with $b=w$, $r=p'$) gives $|ap'|=\Delta$. Symmetrically there is a $\Dt$-neighbour $b$ of $p'$ with $|bp|=\Delta$. Now $|pb|=|ap'|=\Delta$ and $|pa|=|bp'|=\Dt$, so $p,b,a,p'$ are concyclic by the observation in \cref{sec:open} (the four points are distinct: $|pa|=\Dt\ne\Delta=|pb|$, and $a\ne p'$, $b\ne p$ since those distances are $\Delta$). For the last sentence: if $p\in L_1$ has exactly three $\Dt$-neighbours, they lie in an open half-plane at $p$, and the middle one in angular order has neighbours of $p$ strictly on both sides of its line to $p$.
\end{proof}

\section{Sets in convex position}\label{sec:convex}

A set $X$ is in \emph{convex position} if every point of $X$ is a vertex of $\conv X$; in particular no three points of $X$ are collinear. Throughout this section $|X|=n$, $m:=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, $N:=\binom n2$, $D=D(X)$ is the number of distinct distances, and $k=k(X)$ is the number of rare distances ($1\le\mult(d)\le n$). A distance is \emph{frequent} if $\mult(d)\ge n+1$. $R_M$ denotes the vertex set of a regular $M$-gon. By Hopf--Pannwitz, $\Delta$ is always rare; Clemen, Dumitrescu and Liu proved $k\ge2$ for $n\ge5$~\cite[Theorem~1.2]{CDL25}. For $n=5$ this is best possible: $R_5$ has $k=2$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:convex-summary}
Let $X$ be a set of $n$ points in convex position with $6\le n\le18$. Then at least three distinct distances each occur at least once and at most $n$ times. The same holds for every even $n\ge6$.
\end{theorem}

The cases have different status:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item $n=6,7,8$: a corollary of published results (\cref{prop:lit678}).
\item even $n\ge10$: \cref{thm:even}, a parity-sharp version of the proof of~\cite[Theorem~1.2]{CDL25};
\item $n=9$: \cref{thm:nine}, from the classification of Erd\H{o}s and Fishburn~\cite{EF96};
\item $n=11,13,15,17$: \cref{thm:1113,thm:1517}, new and computer-assisted. The $n=7$ case is re-proved the same way, without the literature.
\end{itemize}
In \cref{sec:almostcoc} we show that sets in convex position with all but $w$ points on one circle have at least $(n-3w+1)/4$ rare distances once $n+w\ge91$.

\subsection{Counting and the literature}

\begin{lemma}[parity-sharp counting]\label{lem:counting}
$(D-k)(n+1)+k\le N$. Consequently $k\ge D-m+1$ whenever $D\ge1$. More precisely, if $n=2m+1$ and $D=m+K$ with $1\le K\le m+1$, then $k\ge K+1$; if $n=2m$ and $D=m+K$ with $1\le K<2m$, then $k\ge K+2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The first inequality holds because frequent distances contribute at least $n+1$ pairs each and rare ones at least $1$. It gives $k\ge(D(n+1)-N)/n=D-\frac{n-1}2+\frac Dn>D-m$, hence $k\ge D-m+1$. For $n=2m+1$ the right side is $K+(m+K)/(2m+1)$, with $0<(m+K)/(2m+1)\le1$. For $n=2m$ it is $K+1+K/(2m)$, with $0<K/(2m)<1$.
\end{proof}

We use the following results from the literature.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item[(Alt)] Altman~\cite{Alt63,Alt72}: $D\ge m$; for odd $n$, $D=m$ only for $R_n$ (see also~\cite[Theorem~1]{Fi95}).
\item[(Fi)] Fishburn~\cite{Fi95}: for even $n\ge8$, $D=n/2$ if and only if $X$ is $R_n$ or $n$ vertices of $R_{n+1}$. For $n=6$ the hexagons with $D=3$ are $R_6$, $R_7$ minus a vertex, and one further hexagon~\cite[Theorem~2]{Fi95}, \cite{EF95b}, with multiplicity profiles $(6,6,3)$, $(5,5,5)$ and $(6,6,3)$ respectively (as recorded in~\cite[\S2]{CDL25}; we re-checked the profiles in exact arithmetic).
\item[(EF96)] Erd\H{o}s--Fishburn~\cite{EF96}: a convex nonagon has exactly $5$ distances if and only if its vertices are $9$ vertices of $R_{10}$ or of $R_{11}$.
\item[(r$_2$)] Erd\H{o}s--Fishburn~\cite[\S3]{EF95b}, quoting Fishburn's technical report~\cite{Fi92}: the largest possible second-largest multiplicity in a convex $n$-gon is $r_2^{(n)}=1,3,5,6,7,8$ for $n=3,\dots,8$.
\end{itemize}
A set on a circle has every distance class of maximum degree at most $2$, hence of size at most $n$; so for cocircular $X$ all distances are rare and $k=D$.

\begin{proposition}[from the literature]\label{prop:lit678}
For $n=6,7,8$ in convex position, $k\ge3$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By (r$_2$), $r_2^{(n)}\le n$ for $n\le8$, so at most one distance is frequent and $k\ge D-1$. By (Alt), $D\ge3$ for $n=6,7$ and $D\ge4$ for $n=8$. So $k\ge3$ unless $n\in\{6,7\}$ and $D=3$. For $n=7$, $D=3$ forces $R_7$ (profile $(7,7,7)$, $k=3$); for $n=6$, (Fi) gives the profiles $(6,6,3)$ and $(5,5,5)$, so $k=3$.
\end{proof}
The value $r_2^{(7)}=7$, and the other values of $r_2^{(n)}$, rest on an unrefereed technical report~\cite{Fi92} quoted in a refereed paper~\cite{EF95b}; for $n=7$ we give an independent proof in \cref{thm:1113}.

\begin{theorem}[even $n$]\label{thm:even}
Let $X$ be in convex position with $n=2m\ge6$. Then $k\ge3$. If $n\ge8$ and $k\le3$, then $D=m+1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By (Alt), $D\ge m$. If $D\ge m+1$, \cref{lem:counting} gives $k\ge3$, and $k\ge4$ if $D\ge m+2$. If $D=m$ and $n=6$, (Fi) gives $k=3$. If $D=m$ and $n\ge8$, then $X$ is $R_n$, with profile $(n,\dots,n,n/2)$, or $n$ vertices of $R_{n+1}$, with profile $(n-1,\dots,n-1)$; in both cases $k=m\ge4$.
\end{proof}
\Cref{thm:even} is essentially the argument of~\cite[Theorem~1.2]{CDL25}, with the counting done with parity. It adds to the literature only for even $n\ge10$.

\begin{theorem}[$n=9$]\label{thm:nine}
Every set of $9$ points in convex position has $k\ge3$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Here $m=4$. If $D=4$, then $X=R_9$ by (Alt) and $k=4$. If $D=5$, then $X$ is cocircular by (EF96) and $k=D=5$. If $D\ge6$, \cref{lem:counting} gives $k\ge3$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{$n=7,11,13,15,17$}{n = 7, 11, 13, 15, 17}: an ordinal reduction and SAT certificates}\label{sec:sat}

Label $X=(p_0,\dots,p_{n-1})$ in convex cyclic order, let $d_1<\dots<d_D$ be the distinct distances, and colour each pair $\{i,j\}$ with $c(ij)=t$ where $|p_ip_j|=d_t$.

\begin{lemma}[quadrilateral inequality]\label{lem:Q}
For $i<j<k<l$ (in cyclic order), $|p_ip_k|+|p_jp_l|>|p_ip_j|+|p_kp_l|$ and $|p_ip_k|+|p_jp_l|>|p_jp_k|+|p_lp_i|$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The diagonals of the convex quadrilateral $p_ip_jp_kp_l$ meet at a point $O$ that lies on neither of the sides $p_ip_j$, $p_kp_l$. So $|p_ip_k|+|p_jp_l|=(|p_iO|+|Op_j|)+(|p_kO|+|Op_l|)>|p_ip_j|+|p_kp_l|$, by the strict triangle inequality. The other inequality is the same.
\end{proof}

\emph{Ordinal form.} Let $x_1\le x_2$ be the two diagonal colours, and $y_1\le y_2$ the colours of one pair of opposite sides. Then it is \emph{not} the case that $x_1\le y_1$ and $x_2\le y_2$, since otherwise $|p_ip_k|+|p_jp_l|\le$ the sum of the two sides. We call a colouring of the pairs of an $n$-cycle's vertex set satisfying this for all $4$-tuples and both pairs of opposite sides a \emph{Q-colouring}. This ordinal use of the quadrilateral inequality goes back to Altman~\cite{Alt63}. Abstracting a convex distance problem to a finite search in this way is the method of Mori\'c and Pritchard~\cite{MP11}, whose Fact~3.1 is \cref{lem:Q}.

Some of our certificates also use a second valid constraint (\emph{Lemma T}): if $w\ne w'$ lie on the same open boundary arc between $p_i$ and $p_j$, then $(c(iw),c(jw))\ne(c(iw'),c(jw'))$, because a point is determined by its distances to $p_i,p_j$ and a side of the line $p_ip_j$.

\begin{lemma}[reduction to profiles]\label{lem:R}
If $k\le2$, then at least $D-2$ colour classes have size at least $n+1$, and $D\le D_{\max}(n)$, the largest $D$ with $(D-2)(n+1)+2\le N$. For $n=7,11,13,15,17$, $D_{\max}=4,6,7,8,9$.
\end{lemma}

Call a Q-colouring using exactly $D$ colours, all of them, with at least $F$ classes of size at least $n+1$, an \emph{$(n,D,F)$ ordinal counterexample}. Since \cref{lem:Q} (and Lemma T) are necessary for geometric realisability, the following suffices to prove $k\ge3$ for a given $n$: \emph{for every $2\le D\le D_{\max}(n)$ there is no $(n,D,D-2)$ ordinal counterexample.} (We check the smaller $D$ even where (Alt) would exclude them, so that no citation is needed; for $D\le4$ we check the stronger statement with $F=0$, i.e.\ no Q-colouring with $D$ colours at all.) No realisation step and no floating-point arithmetic enters.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:1113}
Every set of $n$ points in convex position with $n\in\{7,11,13\}$ has at least three rare distances. For these $n$, the ordinal quadrilateral inequality (\cref{lem:Q}) alone implies the statement.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (computer-assisted)]
By \cref{lem:R} it suffices that the following instances have no ordinal counterexample:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item $n=7$: $(7,2,0)$, $(7,3,1)$, $(7,4,2)$;
\item $n=11$: $(11,2,0)$, $(11,3,0)$, $(11,4,0)$, $(11,5,3)$, $(11,6,4)$;
\item $n=13$: $(13,2,0)$, $(13,3,0)$, $(13,4,0)$, $(13,5,0)$, $(13,6,4)$, $(13,7,5)$.
\end{itemize}
Each is encoded in CNF and shown unsatisfiable, with the refutation checked as described below and in \cref{app:verification}.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection*{Encoding}
Variables $x_{e,c}$ say that pair $e$ has colour $c$; there are exactly-one constraints per pair and at-least-one constraints per colour. In the \emph{direct} encoding of \cref{lem:Q}, each dominated $4$-tuple of colours is forbidden. In the \emph{order} encoding, auxiliary literals $y_{e,t}\Leftrightarrow c(e)\ge t$ are introduced and ``$c(a)\le t\le c(b)$ and $c(a')\le u\le c(b')$'' is forbidden for both diagonal/side matchings; this is equivalent, since sorted domination holds if and only if some matching dominates. Selector variables $f_c$ imply ``class $c$ has at least $n+1$ pairs'' through sequential counters, and $\sum_cf_c\ge F$.

\subsubsection*{Certification}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{DRAT/LRAT.} For every instance with $n=11$ and $n=13$ listed above, and for $(7,4,2)$, a DRUP proof produced by Glucose~4~\cite{glucose} was verified by \texttt{drat-trim}~\cite{drattrim}, which also emitted an LRAT proof that was re-verified by \texttt{lrat-check}~\cite{lrat}. The largest certificate, for $(13,7,5)$, has $446{,}686$ lemmas (a 37\,MB DRUP proof and an 82\,MB LRAT proof). For $n=13$ all instances were certified in the Q-only encoding. For $n=11$, $(11,6,4)$ was certified in the Q-only encoding ($35{,}510$ lemmas), and $(11,2..5,\cdot)$ in the encoding with Q and Lemma~T (Q-only is covered by the Lean proof below). A truncated proof is correctly rejected. On this route, the trusted components are the CNF encoder (a short Python program, cross-validated as in \cref{app:verification}) and the proof checkers.
\item \emph{$n=7$ without SAT.} A plain backtracking enumeration of all colourings, with Q checked as soon as its pairs are assigned, finds no ordinal counterexample for $D=2,3,4$. For $D=4$ it does so with Lemma~T switched off.
\item \emph{Lean~4.} The statements for $n=7$, $11$ and $13$ are formalised in Lean~4~\cite{lean4} with Mathlib~\cite{mathlib}; see below.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{$n=15$ and $n=17$}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:1517}
Every set of $n$ points in convex position with $n\in\{15,17\}$ has at least three rare distances. For these $n$, too, the ordinal quadrilateral inequality (\cref{lem:Q}) alone implies the statement.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (computer-assisted)]
Here $m=7,8$ and $D_{\max}=m+1$ (\cref{lem:R}). It suffices to refute, for $n=15$, the instances $(15,D,0)$ for $2\le D\le6$ and $(15,D,D-2)$ for $D=7,8$; and for $n=17$, the instances $(17,D,0)$ for $2\le D\le7$ and $(17,D,D-2)$ for $D=8,9$. Only \cref{lem:Q} is encoded (order encoding, without Lemma~T). The instances with $F=D-2$ are split by the pair $\{r_1,r_2\}$ of colours that are \emph{not} required to be frequent: in the case $\{r_1,r_2\}$ every other colour class is required to have at least $n+1$ pairs, the colours $r_1,r_2$ are required to be used, and the implied bound $|r_1|+|r_2|\le N-(D-2)(n+1)$ is added as a redundant constraint. Every ordinal counterexample falls under at least one of the $\binom D2$ cases. All $5+21+28$ cases for $n=15$ and all $6+28+36$ cases for $n=17$ are unsatisfiable; each refutation was produced by the solver Kissat~\cite{kissat} as a DRAT proof and verified by \texttt{drat-trim}~\cite{drattrim}.
\end{proof}

\emph{Symmetry breaking.} For $D\ge m$ most case encodings contain symmetry-breaking constraints, justified as follows. Q-colourings, and the case conditions, are invariant under the dihedral group acting on $\{0,\dots,n-1\}$: reversing the cyclic order maps diagonals to diagonals and pairs of opposite sides to pairs of opposite sides. (Reversing the order of the colours is not a symmetry, and is not used.) Let $L^*$ be the largest cyclic length $\min(j-i,\,n-j+i)$ of a pair of the largest colour $D$. After a rotation we may assume that $\{0,L^*\}$ has colour $D$; this is encoded by selector variables $s_L$ with $s_L\Rightarrow c(0,L)=D$ and ``no pair of colour $D$ has cyclic length $>L$'', and $\bigvee_L s_L$. The reflection $i\mapsto L-i\pmod n$ fixes $\{0,L\}$, preserves cyclic lengths and exchanges the pairs $\{0,L+1\}$ and $\{L,n-1\}$, so we may also require $s_L\Rightarrow c(0,L+1)\le c(L,n-1)$. As a sanity check, all solutions of two satisfiable instances with lowered thresholds were enumerated and grouped into dihedral orbits ($392$ solutions in $42$ orbits for $n=7$, $D=4$; $21{,}348$ solutions in $1{,}280$ orbits for $n=9$, $D=5$); every orbit meets the symmetry-breaking constraints.

\emph{Cost and trust.} The hard cases are those whose non-frequent pair contains the diameter colour $D$: for $n=17$, $D=9$ these took $3$ to $12$ minutes each, and the largest DRAT proof has about $124$\,MB. The other cases took seconds. Three cases ($(15,8,\{1,2\})$, $(15,7,\{3,7\})$, $(17,9,\{2,3\})$) were re-solved with CaDiCaL~\cite{kissat} and re-verified with \texttt{drat-trim}. The same pipeline re-certifies the cases $D=m+1$ for $n=7,9,11,13$. On this route the trusted components are the CNF encoder (whose Q part is the order encoding validated in \cref{app:verification}), the symmetry-breaking argument above, and \texttt{drat-trim}; the solver is not trusted. The proofs are not stored (they are regenerated deterministically by the scripts), and \cref{thm:1517} is \emph{not} part of the Lean formalisation.

\subsubsection*{The Lean formalisation}
The file \texttt{Erdos/Erdos/E132Convex.lean} proves, without \texttt{sorry},
\begin{center}
\texttt{theorem erdos132\_convex7 (p : Fin 7 $\to$ EuclideanSpace $\R$ (Fin 2)) (hp : StrictConvexPolygon p) : 3 $\le$ (rareDists p).card}
\end{center}
and the analogous \texttt{erdos132\_convex11} and \texttt{erdos132\_convex13} (file \texttt{E132Convex13.lean}) for \texttt{Fin 11} and \texttt{Fin 13}. Here \texttt{StrictConvexPolygon p} means that \emph{every} index-increasing triple $(p_i,p_j,p_k)$, $i<j<k$, is positively oriented; that is, the vertices are given as a strictly convex polygon listed counter-clockwise. \texttt{rareDists p} is the set of distances realised by pairs $i<j$ with multiplicity at most $n$. The proof formalises \cref{lem:Q} (via the intersection point of the diagonals and strict convexity of the Euclidean norm), the passage to ranks and Q-colourings, \cref{lem:R}, and the correctness of a bit-vector encoding of the finite statement ``every Q-colouring with colours below $D_{\max}$ has at least three classes of size in $[1,n]$''. This covers all $D\le D_{\max}$ at once, with unused colours allowed. The finite statement is closed by Lean's \texttt{bv\_decide}, which bit-blasts the formula with a verified bit-blaster, calls the bundled SAT solver CaDiCaL, and checks the resulting LRAT certificate with a checker that is proved correct in Lean.

\emph{Trust base.} \texttt{\#print\allowbreak{} axioms} reports, for each theorem, the standard axioms \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice}, \texttt{Quot.sound}, plus one axiom generated by \texttt{bv\_decide} (\texttt{\dots\_native.\allowbreak bv\_decide.\allowbreak ax\_1\_14}). That axiom asserts that the verified LRAT checker \texttt{verifyBVExpr} returns \texttt{true} on the concrete formula and certificate. The value \texttt{true} is obtained by running compiled code, not by kernel reduction, so the trust base is comparable to that of \texttt{native\_decide}: it includes the Lean compiler and runtime. Neither our Python encoder, nor any external SAT solver, nor \texttt{drat-trim} is trusted on this route. An attempt to check the $n=7$ axiom statement by kernel reduction ran for more than 20 minutes without finishing and was stopped.

\emph{Unordered point sets.} The file \texttt{E132ConvexFinset.lean} proves (using only the three standard axioms) that a finite set $S$ in convex position, in the sense of Mathlib's \texttt{ConvexIndependent}, can be listed as a \texttt{StrictConvexPolygon}. The proof sorts $S\setminus\{p_0\}$ by orientation about a point $p_0\in S$; a negatively oriented increasing triple would put a point of $S$ inside a triangle spanned by others. It also proves that the rare distances of $S$ (unordered pairs) coincide with \texttt{rareDists p} for any such listing. This gives \texttt{erdos132\_convex\{7,11,13\}\_finset}: for \texttt{S : Finset} with \texttt{S.card} $=n$ and \texttt{ConvexIndependent}, $3\le$ \texttt{(rareDistsS S).card}.

\emph{The layer bound.} The results of \cref{sec:layers} are formalised in \texttt{E132Layer.lean} without \texttt{sorry}, and \texttt{\#print\allowbreak{} axioms} reports only \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice} and \texttt{Quot.sound} (no \texttt{bv\_decide} or \texttt{native\_decide}). This covers the localisation of $\Dt$-pairs to $L_1\cup L_2$, the degree-$2$ property of $L_2$-vertices in the $2$-core, the bound $\deg\le4$ on hull vertices, Theorem~A, Corollary~A1 and its unconditional form. There $L_1$ is the set of extreme points of $X$, shown equal to the vertex set of $\operatorname{conv}X$, and the $2$-core is the largest subset with minimum degree at least $2$.

\emph{The $\frac{54}{37}$ bound.} The $\frac{54}{37}$ bound for Problem~1.6 of~\cite{CDL25}, proved in \cref{app:lean54}, is formalised in full in \texttt{E132Main*.lean}, \texttt{E132ConvexCurve.lean} and \texttt{E132Ve87.lean} (about 7{,}000 lines):
\begin{center}
\texttt{theorem erdos132\_main : $\exists$ C\textsubscript{0} : $\R$, $\forall$ X : Finset Pt, 2 $\le$ X.card $\to$ (min (mult X (dist2 X)) (mult X (minDist X)) : $\R$) $\le$ 54/37 * X.card + C\textsubscript{0}}
\end{center}
with \texttt{mult X d} the number of unordered pairs of distinct points of $X$ at distance $d$, and \texttt{dist2}, \texttt{minDist} the second-largest and smallest distance. So the formal theorem \texttt{erdos132\_main} is the $\frac{54}{37}$ bound; the $\frac{15}{11}$ bound of \cref{thm:main15}(a) is formalised separately (below). The formal proof follows the argument of \cref{app:lean54}: \cref{sec:setup,sec:bad,sec:degenerate} and Region~I as in the text, the ownership and pairing argument in Region~II, and the weighted \cref{lem:lp2} in Region~III. There is no \texttt{sorry}, and \texttt{\#print\allowbreak{} axioms} reports only \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice} and \texttt{Quot.sound}. The formalisation also proves Vesztergombi's bound $\mult(\Dt)\le\frac32n$ and the Hopf--Pannwitz bound, so no result is used as a black box. In three places it takes a different route from the text: (a) Vesztergombi's bound is proved in the $2$-core (every degree-$4$ core vertex has two degree-$2$ core neighbours, then double counting), with no point deletion; (b) the bad-edge bounds of \cref{sec:bad} are obtained by counting over directions of outward normals instead of arc length, so \cref{lem:lenscorner} (lens-corner arc length) is not needed; (c) the degenerate-regime incidence bound uses a local charging argument, and the mixed-T bound of \cref{lem:mixedT} is proved in a weaker form (at most $1200$ instead of $71$), which only changes the constant $C_0$.

\emph{The $\frac{15}{11}$ bound, \cref{thm:main15}\textup{(a)}.} The bound $\frac{15}{11}$ is formalised in the six files \texttt{E132Main15*.lean} (about 5{,}300 lines), which build on the $\frac{54}{37}$ development without changing it:
\begin{center}
\texttt{theorem erdos132\_main15 : $\exists$ C\textsubscript{0} : $\R$, $\forall$ X : Finset Pt, 2 $\le$ X.card $\to$ (min (mult X (dist2 X)) (mult X (minDist X)) : $\R$) $\le$ 15/11 * X.card + C\textsubscript{0}}
\end{center}
There is no \texttt{sorry}; \texttt{\#print\allowbreak{} axioms} reports only \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice} and \texttt{Quot.sound}, and the compiled modules pass \texttt{leanchecker}, which re-checks every declaration with the kernel. The set-up, the reductions, \cref{sec:bad}, Region~I, the corner lemma, (R1), \cref{lem:mixedT} and \cref{sec:degenerate} are taken from the $\frac{54}{37}$ development; \cref{lem:identity,lem:lpnew}, \cref{lem:B,lem:C}, the count in Region~II, \cref{sec:regionIII} and \cref{thm:N1} are new.
